picked by the very filthiest of people. Nearly all fruit to be had in the shops, is open to this objection, as anyone will readily admit who has seen a colony of fruit pickers which has been drafted into the country It is one of the privileges of great men and philo- from the slums of London, or other great cities. As a sophers, that they may say things which set the world rule they are of the lowest and dirtiest class imagin- thinking, but which if said by ordinary people would , able, but there is a limit to all things, and when it is be regarded as simply stupid and silly. One is rather . all worked off, we can only hope that the dirt and filth inclined to take this view of the statement of a leading is not enough to contaminate the great bulk of the American physician, that the human race is degenetat- fruit that is offered for sale. That seems to be the only ing because women have taken in recent years to study, security, but at the same time, it it best to wash all and the higher training and culture. Then again a fruit popular French novelist said the other day that mascu- that will stand cleansing in water. line and feminine beauty no longer exists in Europe. I Frenchwomen, he declares, are never beautiful. They I may be pretty, and engaging in looks, but this he considers, is due to their consummate art in matters of dress, arrangement of the hair,and so forth. The answer to all such critics is, of couise, that the girls of the present day are healthier, better made, and better look- ing than were those of past generations. This is just as true in France as in this country, and though there are I some young people who give themselves to overstudy, it is nonsense to say that culture and physical degenera- , tion go hand in hand. Flats and Ruined Tempers.—Many young couples have attributed the failure of married life to the mis- take of taking a" charming little flat." They hope to secure home comforts with a minimum of household I cares, and only learn later on when tempers have become sadly impaired, that living in flats very often means living in a constant state of irritation and warfare with one's fellow beings. Die people who stamp about over- head, the pianist at her scales, on the right, the vocalist on the left, the barking dog below, and bawling children everywhere, all are a maddening source of annoyance, which, in course of time, makes the most amiable of sufferers short tempered and irritable. It is therefore not surprising that the demand for flats should be fall- ing off, and that many enthusiasts who once looked to them for the solution of domestic and household ills, do so no longer. If flats could be made really sound- proof, and free from the annoyance of tramping feet and banging of doors, they would be habitable, but look- ing at them as a whole, it is well to avoid them as being conducive to incompatibility of temper. The Ugly Motor Dress.—The motor car is not itself a thing of beauty, but that is no reason why motorists generally should affect an unnecessarily hideous garb. At some of the fashionable seaside resorts in America, the police have forbidden them from wearing masks, or protruding goggles, and i motorists will persist in turn- ing themselves into child-frighteners, people will begin to think that we shall need similar restrictions over here. Men are the wont sinners, but many women also, make objects of themselves, though there is really no need to adopt a disfiguring style of special clothing. It is certain that neither tailors or modistes have yet devised a becoming automobile equipment, but motorists - might at least refrain from going to the hideous extremes now so frequently seen in the streets. The Importance of Colours.—The long spell of hot and blazing weather we have had this summer, ought to do something to teach us the art of harmonizing the cool, and kindly colours. That it is not too well under- stood is often painfully apparent. Who, for instance has not experienced some feeling of resentment against the woman dressed in startling hues, which presents a, glaring contrast to her hair, and complexion, and carry- ing perhaps, a crimson or purple sunshade. Such a spectacle on a very hot day is enough to set anyone of artistic tastes in a perspiration, but how different it is with the woman who dresses in perfect taste, and well studied regard for the sympathetic scheme of colour. She, on the other hand, will east a sense of coolness all around her, and very often the mot, simple and inex- pensive toilettes are the greatest success, if adapted in perfect taste, to these important details. Concerning Coiffures.—Fashion is as elastic in the matter of hairdressing,as it is in millinery,and provided one keeps somewhere within bounds, one may dress one's hair, very much as one pleases. To prove a success however, the style fixed upon dust accord with features, height, and style, in fact with the whole personality; also it should hide defects. Fringes are out of favour, but a fringe should be worn in the case of a high or receding forehead. Further, if a head is too flat on top the hair should be waved and turned back over a rounded cushion; if a face is too narrow, the hair should be kept low, and well puffed out at the sides. Should the ears be large, or too prominent, the offending members must be hidden by means of softly waved loops of hair. The classic style as seen in Grecian statues, has been fre- quently observed of late, but when we come to consider that it demands a perfect profile, faultless outlines, and a neck that is full, white and rounded, and that few women are blessed to this extent, it is not to be marvel- ledat that the idea has not always proved a success. Large Hats Undesirable at the Seaside.—The picture hat and sea breezes cannot be said to go well together. the result usually being a dishevelled head and a bad ache therein. Tied down with a veil, the large floppy hat is just possible wear at the seaside, but then we are leavin .1, the veil severely alone just now, moreover it has been far too hot and close to swathe our faces in clouds of chiffon, tulle, or gauze. To see large picture hats rearing wildly in the breeze, anxious to float away like miniature and highly decorated air ships, and held on to their mooring of hair and pads by long and dangerous-looking pins, makes one hot and out of all sympathy with the wearers who afford so undesirable and uncomfortable a spectacle. Rosy Cheeks.—The colour comes and goes, and one's maid can fetch and carry it, as Lady Teazle said of her dear friend, but some of the beauty specialists have introduced a new method of producing rosy cheeks, which dispenses with old-fashioned rouge. It is accom- plished by means of tatooing,and has the advantage that the "roses" will not wear off. But it is more than prob- able that they will, in course of time change into a very undesirable shade of mauve, a decided misfortune one would think. On the whole, rouge would seem to be preferable, but it is still better to avoid all artificial aids to beauty,rd try and cultivate, by out-door exercise and careful living, the natural roses of good health. Eating Uncooked Fruit.—One of the medical journals tells us first of all that the strawberry is nearly all water, and of little value from a dietatic point of view, and then that it is not safe to eat, because it is usually

MADGE.

DAIILED GIRLS.

Story of an Adventurous Career.

William Thomas, the man whose arrest on charges of swindling women resulted in releaseanda King's pardon for Mr. Adolph Beck, has told the police a remarkable story of his past career. Beck has already , served five years for frauds which he had not committed. The story related by Thomas was re-told on Tuesday at Bow-street. According to Detective Kane Thomas in his state- ment, said his proper name was William Augustus Wyatt. He was born at Sturton, near Horncastle, Lincolnshire, in 1839. His father was a man of some means. , He then proceeded to give a sketch of ,his career, from which it appeared that be had when young, lived in Russia had studied medicine at Vienna, gaining degrees of M.D. and M. 0., and that when war broke out between Prussia and Austria in 1866 he joined the Austrian Army as a volunteer surgeon. At the conclusion of the war he went to America, and travelled about the States, practising as an oculist. In 1876 he visited England, lodging at Islington until his arrest in 1877. On his liberation from prison in 1381 he went to America, and continued the occupation of an oculist. Some time about the beginning of the nineties he • went to the South Sea Islands. Evidence was given by several young women. Evelyn Edwards, a waitress, for instance,said the accused spoke to her, and asked her if she would like to live in St. John's Wood. He drew an alluring picture of thousands of pounds' worth of jewels, and at last, dazzled with the proposal, she consented. He gave her a cheque, and tried unsuccessfully to borrow a ring. On trying to cash the cheque she found that the prisoner was unknown at the bank. Then she informed the police. Another girl, who parted with a ring, was promised £2OO to purchase dresses in order to accompany him on a yachting trip. When he practised in Adelaide, as Dr. Meyer, prisoner —according to Mr. Day, a watchmaker, who formerly lived there—kept a carriage and servants. Prisoner, who had nothing to say to the magistrate was committed for trial.

VEGETABLES UP-TO-DATE.

The strict vegetarian and all others who appreciate the very useful part which vegetables play in our d .ily dietary, must often wish for a means of making the homelier vegetables more appetising. A simple and most successful way to accomplish this, is to serve the vegetables with an appetising sauce. Such sauces are easily made with the aid of Brown and Poison's " Patent " Corn Flour. Brown Sc Poison's publish a special recipe book entitled "Fish and Vegetable Sauces," a copy of which will be forwarded to every lady sending a id. stamp to B. Sz. 14., Paisley. The recipes include sauces for even the most common vegetables in daily use, such as carrots,turnips.cabbage, &c., and to serve such vegetables with a good sauce gives them an added relish.

INTERESTING GRASS EXPERIMENTS.—The grass experiments of Mr. Ernest Park at Kineton, in Warwick- shire have now yielded their third annual record. The fields are on old grass land originally poor from long repeated mowing and grazing without return—land locally regarded as hopelessly poor by nature,and typical of much neglected grass land throughout the country. In one field, by the advice of Dr. Dyer, who is superintending the experiments, superphosphate is used as a phosphatic manure, and basic slag in the other, this difference being due to natural differences in the proportion of lime in the soils. Some plots are left • unmanured, others treated every year with basic slag or superphosphate as the case may be, with and without nitrate of soda; while one plot in each field receives nitrate only. Potash salts also have been applied to some plots, but so far potash does not seem to be needed. The natural yield of the land on one field this year was but llcwt of hay per acre. Basic slag alone (scwt. per acre) raised the crop to one ton 18cwt., nitrate alone (2cwt. per acre) to 2 tons 3cwt., while basic slag and nitrate together pro- duced 2 tons 9cwt., giving a gain of 1 ton 18cwt. per acre at a cost of about 355. for manure. On the other field the natural yield was only 81cwt. of hay per acre, even in this prolific season. Superphosphate alone (3cwt. per acre) raised the crop to 1 ton 14csvt., nitrate of soda alone (2cwt. per acre), to 2 tons, and a combination of both manures to 2 tons -Icwt., making an increase of 1 ton 15cwt. of hay per acre at a cost of about 30s. for manure. The herbage on all the phosphate plots, whether slag or superphosphate, is very rich in clover, while where nitrate is used without any phosphate it is more grassy in character. The best herbage all round as judged by the eye before mowing, was that on the plots getting both phosphates and nitrate. Quite apart from the increase in bulk, the herbage on all the manured plots, even after only three years of treatment, appears to be quite different from that of the original poor weedy pasture which is presarved for comparison on the unmanured plots.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Examiner Saturday, August 6, 1904.

(Comiright.)

"Wait here for me," she said as she alighted. She crossed the pavement and pushed open the iron gate. She glanced up at the doorway, then drew back with a slight start of suprise. Standing with his back towards her, in the act of slipping a latch-key into the lock, was Mr. Carl Smith. His tall frame nearly filled the small suburban doorway. As the gate creaked on its hinges he swung round and faced her. A smile spread over his face as he recogni3ed her. He raised his hat and took a step towards her bolding out his hand. "This is really very good of you," he said, frankly. Nor,. four d herself returning his grasp as if he had bet... the friend of a lifetime, instead of the chance acquaintance of yesterday. "You have come to see how the child is ? " She nodded. He pushed open the door and stood aside for her

to pass. "If you do not mind coming in, you will be able to jri.-Jge for yourself." He shut the front door and led the way into a room opening from the small hall. A kindly- looking elderly woman appeared on the scene. "This lady has come to see how the little girl is, Mrs. Biggs," said Mr. Smith. "It was my carriage, you know, that nearly ran over er," said Nora. "Is she much hurt ? " "ar, no miss !" returned Mrs. Biggs. "The doctor said her arm was bruised a bit, and put a , bandage round it, but otherwise she's all right. ' There she is.,, Nora entered the room. At the further end, sitting on a couch with a gaily coloured pictura• book spread before her, was the child. "Looks a bit different, to yesterday," remarked Mrs. Poiggs, with a chuckle of pride. " I washed her and put her into some of my little Nelliev. things." • Nora hurried forward and bent :down before tr!va, child. "Why, she's quite pretty," she exclaimed, tut-D- -ing round to Mr. Smith. "Yes," he said, with a smile; "amongst tb,l lower.. classes ugliness is frequently only dirt

deep." On catching sight of him the child had allowed the picture-book to drop to the floor, and a beam of delight spread over her face. She stretched out the unbandaged arm towards him. He moved forward and dropped on one knee before her. Mrs. Biggs retired quietly from the room, shutting the door behind her. "Queer little thing, isn't she ? " he said. glancing up at Nora. "Yes. Have you found out anything about her ? "

"Not yet. Mrs. Biggs couldn't get anything 1 out of her. But I've given notice to the police,. and, doubtless, they'll soon let me know." The door opened and Mrs. Biggs re-appeared. I bearing a tea-tray laden with things. "I had just got your tea, sir," she said, with glance at Mr. Smith, "and I thought that perb4..— the young lady—" Mr. Smith looked inquiringly at Nora. "It's very kind of you," she said, half-shyly. The spirit of adventure was strong on her. "Capital!" he cried, and Mrs. Biggs busied away for the teapot. Nora's eyes wandered round the room. There were two large bookcases crammed with what looked like scientific works. In one corner was a large writing- table strewn with papers, and leaning against the wall was a drawing-board on which were pinned some plans. "That's a suggested improvement for coal bunkers," he saki, with a smile, as he noted what she was looking at. She turned to him quickly. "Are you an inventor?" she exclaimed. "Hardly that. I'm a constructor in the Penrosse Works, a builder of ships." "Battleships ?" she asked, eagerly. "Yes. At present I'm helping with a cruiser for the Japanese Navy. We're just finishing, her." "I love battleships," she exclaimed, with a gleam in her eye. "The Navy is my special craze." "Is it ?" he cried. "Well, then, I should like you to come down to the works, and ask Sir George if I might shew you over." "Would you?" she exclaimed, delightedly, Then an idea occurred to her. "We're going to Sir George's reception on Friday. I'll ask him then."

He broke into a laugh. "That will be splendid." Nora caught sight of the . little clock on the mantelpiece. The hands pointed to half-past five. "Oh, I must be going," she exclaimed. "I had no idea it was so late.' "The time has simply flown," he said, regret- fully. He moved towards the door and held it open. "You'll let me know if you find out about the parents of the child ? " she said, suddenly. He had one hand on the handle of the door. As she spoke he turned and their eyes met. Then they both laughed. "I'm afraid I had forgotten the child for the moment," he said. He opened the front door and shewed her into the cab that was still waiting. "It was awfully good of you," he began, as he held out his hand. "I was so anxious," she replied; and, somehow, they both laughed again. "Good-bye," he said, "that is, until you come to see the things that will be ships." "I shall come," she said, firmly. "Good-bye." He closed the doors in front of her, then drew back on the pavement. She caught one last glimpse of the tall, bare-headed figure, then the cab swung round and the next moment he was lost to view. She rode home as if in a dream. Arriving at Gilthorpe House she dressed hurriedly, then set out in her own carriage for the Denesons', where she had promised to dine. FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

Many people are leaving town, for the in- tense heat has caused a general exit of all those who can possibly transfer the scene of their daily duties and social life to the seaside or country, where a cooler atmosphere and fresher breezes prevail and life is much more comfortable. Far be it, however, for us to grumble at the lovely weather with which we have been blest, for we have not experienced anything for some years which so nearly attains to the blissful warmth and sunshine of an old - fashioned summer which supplies gratified femininity with countless oppor- tunities of displaying to advantage dainty summer toilettes of muslin and lace. Indeed, there have been far more light-coloured gowns en evidence this year than some sea- Sons have seen, and white has had quite a remarkable vogue in all sorts of materials varying from a substantial ribbed serge suitable for yachting or boating to the most diaphanous of finely embroidered imiSlins, which, by their exquisite beauty, remind one

of the princess's dress in the fairy tale, which could be drawn through a finger ring. Serge gowns are most useful for they do not soil easily, and when well fitted and cut look at their best cut on the simplest of lines. Pipings in contrasting colours are often used on gowns of this style to finish the seams on bodice and skirt, while coarse makes of lace form the ornamental collars or deep yokes. Finer lace is, however, used on soft muslin as in my first sketch, which shows a simple toilette of muslin trimmed with rows of valenciennes lace insertion. It is completed by a wide brimmed hat trimmed with roses, all being enveloped in a white veil spotted with black chenille. 'One of the fashionable feather stoles forms a pretty wrap with its curling white feathers. There are many dainty additions to dresses such as this, and, while feathers are perhaps the most generally favoured, there are some charming silk or crepe de chine scarves patterned with a blurred floral design and finished by a fringe intermingled with fine gold threads, which are also interwoven through the material. These are generally worn drawn round the figure just below the droop of the shoulders and are either tied in front or in other cases are care- lessly draped and caught round the arms. Some, of course, are made of lace but these are more costly than the others and are therefore not so generally worn as it is not everybody

who can afford these luxuries. These are a few ruffles of taffetas to be seen but these seem to have been considered not so elegant as the newer and therefore more fashionable

wraps. I have rarely seen a prettier or smarter blouse than that depicted in my illustration. It is made of rose-pink silk with a handsome lace yoke and numerous ruchings of the silk. The sleeves are made in a novel, design with the fulness caught in a pnif at the top and then falling in a frill edged with the silk ruching over the full undersleeve, which is again caught into a very wide wristband of lace. There is no collar, but the blouse is cut a little low in the form of a V at the throat, this giving it a very dressy effect. The front of the bodice is drawn into the yoke and the fulness is again confined at the waist by a wide silk band. The yoke is, of course, long on the shoulders for no really smart blouse is seen this season without this feature. A muslin dress would make up very prettily in this style with the skirt slightly kauged rd'und the hips and made with an over-dress or pannier edged with a ruche of silk and falling over a frill of the muslin. The bodice could also be finished with silk ruches though, of course, muslin treated in the same way could be substituted, though with not such a good effect. Nowadays we always

A PRETTY HAT.

send our muslin gowns when soiled to the dry cleaners rather than *trust them to the tender mercies of the ,laundry, which is apt to prove disastrous and rather too severe in treatment for the delicate materials, the former treat- ment being preferable if a little more expen-

, sive. Hats are still most picturesque in design and boast beautifully curved brims and rather higher crowns than we have of late seasons been accustomed to. The simple but pretty hat given in my sketch lias a wide shady brim trimmed with pink and white gauze ribbon, which forms a chou at one side and also wide strings which are brought round and tied under, the chin. It is a charming though simple piece of headgear and would look well with any sort of dress whether serge or muslin. The shape of the hat could be copied in fine chip if a rather dressy hat were required, or coarse rustic straw for a morning country hat. The high jam-pot crown has become very popular. I recently saw an exceedingly pretty hat of this kind,-made of fine black chip and trimmed with a single black ostrich feather which was arra-nged a little j to one side of the front with the end of the plume curling over the brim. Black velvet ' strings were brought from behind and form the only other trimming except an ornamen- tal buckle securing the plume and apparently holding it in exactly the right position. COVETTE;

THE EXTRAVAGANT SITUATION

DY GILBERT DAYLE,

Author of "The Fates and Prince Conrad," "Tlic Man from Parenna," "Princess Copra," B:.c.

"You know, Nora, it's rather absurd," the Honourable Miss Ella Deneson. WaS remarking. "here you are, in your twenty-second year, and not in love yet!" Her companion, . a bright, pretty-looking girl, laughed. "WS a mistake falling in love too early, or too often.," she replied, as she rose from her chair and glanced out of the window. Gilthorpe House faced Hyde Park, and the big &retch of trees and green looked particularly attractive in the bright sunshine of a spring after- noon. She stared reflectively at the scene for a moment, then turned with a smile to her friend.

"But when I do, there will be no mistake ! One day I shall see a man—perhaps wallOng across there ! " she indicated the Park with a gesture the hand, "and I shall know at once that he o the--"

"My dear Nora," interrupted Ella. reprovingly, "that savours of romance! And in these days, anti in your position-" "The title shan't keep me from marrying the man I love," broke in the independent young person. Ella Deneson rose and slipped her arm throrgit Nora's. •

"1 shall have to tell Archie to walk in the Park," said she.

Nora laughed and shook her head. "He'll be home on Wednesday," continued Ella, "he is coining round by sea instead of overland from Marseilles. Why, I don't know." "Probably an attraction on board," said Nora, with a smile.

"I wish you'd marry him! " exclaimed Ella, abruptly. "It would be such a comfortable arrange- ment."

Nora looked doubtful,

"You have such peculiar ideas about love," Ella continued. "I'm afraid you have been reading too many novels." Nora shook her head.

"No: I've only got an idea that I would like to marry a man I really love." She looked up into •her friend's face. "It may be Archie after all, but you mustn't think me horrid if it isn't. And besides, he hasn't asked me, you know." "He will before he is home tin- days," said thr Honourable Miss Deneson, with an air of certainty• A clock on the mantelpiece chimed three. "I must be going," said Ella, with a sigh. "I promised to look in at Mrs. Kenishain's Fancy Fair. And Nora," she continued, "whether it's Archie or not, you'll tell me at once when it does happen, won't you? " "Yes, I'll do that," said Nora, with a laugh. Left to herself, Nora proceeded to prepare for her drive, and a few minutes afterwards was seated in an open victoria. She watched the people with interest; everybody looked cheerful and bright in the May sunshine. Presently the carriage turned into a quiet street, at the end of which was Cresden-avenue, where Lady Berk port resided. Glancing ahead, her (ye lighted upon a little ragged child toddling at the side of the road. Then she noticed a man just turning the corner, and it struck her that he was exceptionally tall; at least six feet two, she idly conjectured. They had nearly reached the corner when she saw the child make a quick little run into the middle of the road. Her back was turned and she had not noticed the carriage coming. The coach- man uttered a loud cry of warning, and attempted to pull the horses up, but the brutes were spirited and difficult to manage. Nora could not see the child, and she drew in her breath as she thought of what might happen. Suddenly, a figure made - quick dash from the pavement under the horses' heads, and the next moment she saw him emerge and gain the pavement. In his arms he held what looked like a big ragged bundle. The carriage came to a standstill, and opening the door, Nora sprang out and made her way to the man with the bundle. As she approached, lie —it was the tall man—glanced up and their

eyes met. "Is she hurt ?" began Nora, anxiously. • "More frightened than really damaged, I think," he replied. "See, she has opened her eyes."

The child stared round in -a frightened manner, then began to whimper dismally. Her left aria hung down limply,by its side. "The off horse touched it as I snatched her

away," he explained. "I expect. it is only bruised." -Nora's eyes drifted from tiv child up to the man's face. lie was fair, had well-cut features, a high forehead and was clean shaven. It .occurred to her that he was the best-looking man ,she had ever met. And he seemed to tower above her.

"You saved her life," she said, slowly, "and risk d your own." -

The man laughed. Opportunity," he said, his eyes meeting her

gaze "What arse you going to do?" asked Nora. "Take the little one home, have a doctor to look at her arm, then try and find out who she is, and ultimately return her to her humble and probably drunken parents." "But it isn't—it isn't right to put you to such 'trouble," she exclaimed. "Let me take her in the carriage and have her attended to." The man glanced first at the elegant carriage now di awn up by the kerb, then at her eager young face. "No, • no," he said, "I have an old woman at my lodgings who will look after her admirably." "But I shall be anxious to know whether she is hurt ; remendior. I feel partly responsible." The man smiled.. • "Could I not send to inquire ?" she continued, quickly ; "that is, if you would not mind," she added. He dived a hand into an inner pocket and brought out an old envelope. "Certainly, if you care," he said. "I have no card, but this will give you my name and address." She took it from hint. He moved towards the cab with the child in his arms. Nora followed hint. He turned with one foot on the step. "Good-bye," he said, with a frank smile. It was only an incident, yet it took a strange hold on her for the rest of the day. The next morning site was in her boudoir at Gilthorpe House, seated at her writing-table. With a pen in one hand she was gazing thoughtfully at a crumpled envelope, on which was written "Mr. Carl Smith, 51, Aplin-road, Chiswick." "Carl," she murmured to herself, thoughtfully "It's a pretty name. But Smith!" She sat thinking for some minutes, then shs, dipped her pen resolutely in the ink and started oil a sheet of paper. "Dear sir," she began, repeating the words to herself. Then she struck the words through, and started a fresh piece of paper. "Dear Mr. Smith," that was her second attempt, but it seemed too familiar. After a little reflection she took a third • piece of paper. "Lady Nora Gilthorpe presents her complifoents to Mr. Carl Smith, and would be much obliged if he would kindly let her know----" She paused for a moment. "It really seems a most awkward letter to write," she exclaimed at, length, throwing down the pen. in disgust. She rose and went to the window. Down in the Park below she could see crowds of well-dressed men taking their morning promenade. She found herself idly studying them. "He would be a head taller than most of them," she said, suddenly. Presently she turned and walked back to her writing-,table to further consider n the note. But it was o good. The letter absolutely refused to be written. Finally she dropped her pen and shut up her writing-case. As site did so, an idea flashed upon her. "It would be much better," sire exclaimed. "A. personal interview is always so much more satis- factory. And papa is away. Not that that matters, for I'm sure he'd approve," she added to herself. reassuringly. "I'll go at once." . But she didn't. It was four in the afternoon before. she finally made up her mind. Then she set out by herself walking, and at Sloane-street she chartered a cab. In a short time she had rcached Hammersmith. The cab drove quickly down the Broadway and presently stopped. But it was only for the driver to inquire his way. "Second on the left, lust to the right, right you are!" she heard him say in husky tones as they started again. 7 Presently they swung round a turning and, glancing up, , she caught sight of the wordl sudden "Aplin-road.' Another fifty yards or so and the cab came to a standstill.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

ASSASSINATION OF RUSSIAN MINISTER.

One of those terrible tragedies which from 'time to time paralyse St. Petersburg occurred :at the end of last week, when M. de Plehve, the Minister of the Interior, was utterly blown to pieces with a bomb. Following as it does so close upon the assassinations of General Bobrikoff and M. Sipiaguine, it has .struck consternation into the hearts of the Russian officials, and it is a serious sign of revolution against the powers that be. These •assassinations are unquestionably the culmina- tion of national discontent, which has been 'brewing for a very long while, and has in no 'Way been soothed by the ill-success of Russia in the war. M. de Plehve, of all the ministers, has made himself the most un- popular by his methods of dealing with the student riots and the Kishineff massacres for Which he was undoubtedly responsible, and It was an open secret that he would be the first to suffer in event of any anti-Govern- ment action. Unquestionably, the whole of Russia is seething internally, and it looks as though nothing could save the country

from a revolution but a great victory, in the Far East, • which grows more and more im- probable every day, for the birth of a son and heir to the Romanoff dynasty, which is seriously assailed, at the approaching con- finement of the Tsarina. The story of M. de Plehve's assassination is briefly this. Every Thursday morning at ten o'clock it was his habit to report in person to the Tsar, and last Thursday on his way, when near the Warsaw Station in St. Petersburg, a bomb was thrown into his carriage by a young man from the Warsaw Restaurant. Death was instantaneous, and the Minister for the Interior, his coachman, horses, and carriage were blown instantaneously into small pieces. The murderer was promptly arrested, and a second bomb was found in his possession. M. de Plehve was a man of iron and ruled with autocratic severity, and his end was only what .might have been expected to follow his policy ,in Russia. Few men were more iib ensely hated.

THE CHANNEL SWIM

Swimming the Channel promises to be the great athletic boom of the summer, and under the auspices of three London papers three swimmers are in full training for the great event. They are Montague Holbein, Jack Haggerty, and Greasley, and each one professes him- self confident of success. The

attempts will take place to:

wards- the end

of August or the

beginning of September, and

there seems a

good prospect that, with all

the careful or-

ganisationwhich

is •being spent

upon tile -sub-

ject, Captain Webb's great

feat

may

'equalled. Haggerty and Greasley are strong swimmers and faster than Holbein, but the latter is the man with the experience. Already he has made four unsuccessful attempts, of which one, that of August 27th, 1902, was a moral victory. He has hitherto failed from the fact that, though strong and of immense endurance, he lacks pace to get across on two tides and is carried back again on the third. To swim the Channel is a most difficult matter owing to tides and currents, and, though only twenty-one miles as the crow flies, it is nearly forty in the Accomplishment, as an angular course has to be steered. Holbein's favourite course is from Dover to Cape Grisnez or from Cape Grisnez .to Dover, and he will again adopt it this year. His magnificent pluck and determination have appealed to the sporting instinct of every Briton, and all good wishes for success go with him. Perhaps this year the spur of competition may give him that ,extra probe he has hitherto lacked.

THE BALTIC FLEET AND THE FAR EAST.

The Baltic Fleet; after much preliminary -flourish of trumpets, appears from all ac- counts to be on the eve of sailing for the Far East in the hope of re-establishing Russian naval prestige. However, four of its finest battleships the Orel, the Borodino, the Kniaz Suvaroff, and the Slava—cannot pos- sibly sail before September at the earliest, and perhaps not until Christmas, by which time it is quite possible that the war may be over. They are each 13,70 r, tons, and up to 18 'knots. Ths fleet in question is to sail in two divisions under the supreme command of Rear-Admiral Rozhdestvensky, of the Naval General Staff; and under him Rear-Admiral

THE COMMANDERS OF THE BALTIC FLEET. Enkvist, who until recently was Governor of the naval dockyard and arsenal of Nicolaieff, on the Black Sea, will command one division, and Rear Admiral Felkersan the other. The fleet consists of the Imperator Alexander 111., 13,516 tons; the Qslyabia, 12,674 tons (a sister ship to the Poltava) ; the Sissoi Velky, an older boat of 8,880 tons, and only a second- class battleship,, hampered by small coal bunkers; and the. Navarin, older still, but slightly larger, being of 9,500 tons displace- ment. The cruisers are the Aurora (6,600), the Svietlana (3,823), the Admiral Nakimoff (7,782), the Dimitri Donskoi (5,900) tons, and the Alruaz (6,520. There is, also, a sufficient complement of torpedo craft and colliers, for coaling will provide the chief difficulty on the way. Many difficulties are ahead of the Baltic Pleet when it moves, but it holds in its keeping Russia's only chance of success.

THE OLDEST MEMBER OF PARLIAMENT'S FEAT.

If another reminder were needed of I=',y liament's all night sitting, which took .pla,e tbe other week, it is forthcoming i.n

pleasant tittle ceremony which took place in the House during the week. This was the presentation of a silver loving cup to Mr. Spencer Charringto oldest member of the House of

Commons, for his prowess in seeing the sit- ting through. The cup was subscribed for by both Conser- vatives a n d Liberals. Mr. Charrington, it

may be men- tioned has represented the Mile End division of the Tower Hamlets since 1886 in the Conservative interest, and ispopular with Liberals as well as with his own party. He is 'B6 years of age and is head of " Char- rington's Brewery," one of the East End s most far seen landmarks. He has made himself very popular with his fellow East- Enders, for it is a thing on which Mr. Charrington greatly prides himself that he is an East - Ender. He is, even now, generally to be found in the daytime at the big business premises that bear his name, and he used to sleep in the East-End. Mr. Charrington's speeches at election time are models that many a Parliamentary candidate would do well to follow—very short, and very much to the point, if wanting something in oratorical fluency. In one respect the Charrington family is not " entire, for Mr. F. N. Charrington, a brother of the subject of our brief sketch, has broken away from his family and sacrificed his interest in the brewery in order so devote himself to good Norks.

A POPULAR CRICKETERS BENEFIT.

• Perhaps no cricketer who has ever played our national game deserved more the benefit which thg Yorkshire County Cricket Club gave George Hirst, the popular professional of this county's famous eleven, in their match with Lancashire which commenced at Leeds on Monday last. George H. Hirst, or " Georgie " as he is familiarly called round Huddersfield way, is without doubt the most popular cricketer of the day, and that he is one of the best all round cricketers playing and has been for many years, will not be denied by anyone. For many years in succession he has been one of the select band of cricketers who make their thousand runs and Jake one hundred wickets, besides which it is not easily forgotten what a deal he has had in the success of the Yorkshire team for the last five or six years. Hirst

===== 0002601_19040806_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
G. H. HIRST.

eiong with the Hon. F. S. Jackson, J. R. Mason, and Braund, has stood in a class entirely different to the majority of cricketers and it is very satisfactory to record fhe fact that his services are being recognised whilst he is at the top of his form, and not when he has run his course. The Yorkshire pro- fessional during the course of his career, which extends over twelve years, has twice visited Australia, first with Mr. A. E. Stod- dart's eleven, and last year with Mr. P. F. Warner's victorious team. Peculiarly to say, he was not what one could call a success on either visit, his first appearance on Australian wickets, indeed, being described by a contem- porary as "only very moderate." Last year's display "down under" did much to remove this stigma, and whilst he did not reap many bowling successes, his batting was one of the features of the tour. Hirst, however, has done many brilliant things in International cricket in this country, the chief of which was, however, his display in the last " test " at the Oval when, along with the Hon. F. S. Jackson, G. L. Jessop, and Rhodes, he helped England to win the most exciting match ever played in this country by one wicket. As showing how they do things in Yorkshire, it may be stated that even if the weather had in any way affected the attendance the financial side of the match would not have been a failure. This is ex- plained by the fact that for many months subscription books had been scattered all over the county, and these were willingly responded. to even by the poorest enthusiast, and therefore whilst the amount, of course, would not have been so large had pleasant conditions prevailed, still the benefit could have been voted a success.

DEATH OF SIR JOHN SIMON

In this age of hygiene Sir John Simon, the pioneer of sanitation, is a name highly honoured on every side, but his death recalls the difficulties, opposition, and blind pre- judice which it fell to his lot to fight in his early days. Born in' IBi6, the distinguished sanitary scien-

tist, who passed

away last -week,

qualified as far back as 1838,

and ten years

later he wajs-a-p-:

pointed • fir'st officer of Healtl

to the City O.

London. In /855 he was

Medical

Officer to the

General Board of Health, and

three years later he became Medical Officer

to the Privy Council, which

JOHN SIMON.

post he held till 1876, combining with it from 1871 onwards the same office with regard to the Local Government Board. As far back as 1845 he had become an F.R.S., and was vice- president in 1879-80, while from 1876 to 1890 he reprennted the Crown on the General Medical Council. Created a C.B. in 1876 and a K.C.B. upon the occasion of the Jubilee, Sir John Simon was a man who did not care for honours, and when, upon his retirement, it was proposed to get up a national testimonial for him he studiously declined. However, a bust of him was placed in the College of Surgeons, and in 1887 the Sanitary Institute published the whole of his Public Health Reports in two volumes, which form a history of the early days of sanitary science. Sir John Simon himself, in 1890, published an inter- esting book• on the subject which is inval- uable to all students of the subject. To him is due all our present system of State medicine and sanitation, and the late scien- tist deserves his., country's warmest gratitudta

THE WISDOM OF GLADSTONE.

The late Mr. Gladstone used -frequently to remark how careless his friend, John Bright, was of his health. It was generally believed that Mr. Bright never sought medical advice ; but this was not quite correct, for the great Liberal orator one day astonished Sir Andrew Clarke with his presence in that well-known physician's consulting room. And yet Mr. Bright lived to be seventy-eight; so that we see, in spite of his alleged carelessness in matters of health, he lived to be an old man, and overlapped the average terra of life. Mr. Gladstone almost climbed up in the nineties—but then Mr. Gladstone was all his life keenly watchful of his health and wisely safe- guarded it. Even if Mr. Bright had make a better record than Mr. Gladstone, still the latter's practice would have been right and the former's wrong. It is the general average that tells the story. In the autumn of 1889 (the year Mr. Bright died), Mrs. M. A. Bathurst, of Yelden Higharn Ferrers, began to realise that her health was failing. She felt low and weak without knowing why. She lost all relish for food, and often after her meagre meals endured much pain at the chest and sides, a tendency which increased until eating changed from a pleasure to a dreaded necessity. After a year, this misery was intensified by an attack of acute rheumatism. "My legs, ankles, and knees were stiff and plinful, and the joints swollen," wrote Mrs. Bathurst on sth October, 1898, "so that I had great difficulty in getting about. I tried many ' medicines, internal and external, but obtained no more than slight temporary relief from the best of them until my attention was called to Mother Seigel's Syrup. That remedy completely rid me both of indigestion and rheumatism, so that I now live and work in I comfort."

As it is sometimes alleged against rapid cures that their good effects are not lasting, we would again refer to the case of Mrs. Bathurst in proof that, however true this may be of many other medicines, it is certainly not true, of Mother Seigel's Syrup. "Six years have now passed," writes,Mrs. Bathurst, on March inth, 1904, "since I was cured of indigestion and rheumatism by Mother Seigel's Syrup, and there has been no return of either complaint. My appetite is good, and I have no need to exercise special care as to what I eat. In fact my general health is excellent, though T still take a dose of the syrup now and agaip as a safeguard against the return of the rheumatism." Mrs. Bathurst's precaution would have commended itself to Mr. Gladstone. who, in matters o! health, would leave nothing to chance.

FAILURES IN ENGLAND AND WALES.—According to "Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, July 29th, was 175, viz.: —New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 69; Deeds of Arrangement registered. 95. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: Bankruptcies, 85; Deeds of Arrangement, 72—Total 157; being a net increase in Bankruptcies 'of 165. and a net increase in Deeds of Arrangement in 1904, to date, of 392.

•./1. "SOUTHERN IRELA.ND."—The Great Western Railway Company has just issued an illustrated book, entitled "Southern Ireland." To holiday seekers the pub liCa- tion should be of great interest. The lakes and land- scapes and the chief points of interest in Southern Ireland are pleasantly described, and what is of more importance to the tourist, the best ways of reaching them are set forth. There is also a useful appendix, giving particulars of train and bat services, coach routes, tourist arrangements and some of the fares in operation as well as a coloured map.

MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

MEETING OF SHAREHOLDERS.

The Recent Litigation.

The half-yearly meeting of the shareholders of the Manchester Ship Canal Company was held on Thursday at. the Midland Hotel. Mr. J. K. Bythell presided. The Chairman spoke of the two bills which the Com- pany have had before Parliament this session. The half year just terminated, he said, would always be remem- bered as a very eventful period, in the history of the Company. They had promoted two bills in Parliament; one had received the Rtyal assent, and the other had been read for the third time in the Upper House, and was therefore sure to become an Act of Parliament before the session closed. These measures would have a very important influence on the future of the under- taking, and would most certainly operate eventually to the material benefit of the shareholders. THE ARRANGENIENT WITH THE CORPORATION. The one which had received the Royal assent exactly carried out the arrangements between the Corporation of Manchester and the Company which were approved by theshareholders in February last. Under this scheme the shareholders would be charged 3 1-5 oar cent per annum instead of 41 per cent on the loan of five millions. The arrangement was retrospective. Before its adop- tion all the arrears of interest, calculated at 4 per cent amounting to £1,800,000, stood as a debt, payable in cash,before the shareholders could receive any dividend. Now, however, all the arrears that had accrued and all that might still accrue would be workee out at 31-5 per cent, and the reduced rate would be no longer payable in cash, but converted to 3 per cent non-cumulative preference shares. (Hear, hear.) He knew that some shareholders thought the Company might have got better terms from the Corporation, but when, after months of close negotiations, the time had come to go to Parliament if they wanted any legislation to take effect in the coming session, he thought the shareholders were very wise in closing with the offer. If they had let it slip, of course the question arose whether similar terms would have been re-opened. .The writing off of a million pounds of arrears—for that was what tilt arrangement would eventually work out to—and the acceptance of 31 per cent non-cumulative preference shares in respect of the balance, certainly did improve the position of the shareholders very materially. (Hear, hear.) It removed a great burden of debt which made the position formerly almost hopeless.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEEPENING OF TEIE CANAL.

The other Act was to empower the Company to increase the depth of the canal from 26ft. to 28ft. That would be done in the estuary section, twenty-one miles in length, from Eastbam to Latchford by retaining two feet more tidal water. That power was granted after a severe struggle in Parliament. From Latchford to Manchester. in consequence of the height of the railway , bridges, it would be necessary to carryout the deepening by dredging,. As soon as the measure had received the Royal assent, the work would go on with all possible speed. They were already making arrangements to begin dredging as soon as the bill was approved. Even at the present time they were badly in want of the extra depth, and it was quite certain that if within a very short time they did not deepen the canal a good deal of the expansion they hoped for would not be retained. Mr. Bythell re-alled the circumstances under which the Ship Canal Company had been put to considerable expenditure in opposing the Trafford Park Dock Bill and in defending the action brought against them by the Warrington Corporation. In mentioning that Warrington were appealing to the House of Lords he said that certain overtures had been made on the part of the company, but without effect. Having disposed of the" special business" of the half year, Mr. Bythell reviewed what he termed the "ordinary" business. The results obtained in the Ship Canal department justified, he said, the hope of con- tinued steady progress. Remembering all the circum- stances and drawbacks, an increase of 54,206 tons and £9.000 in receipts compared very'encouragingly with a half year whi,.h showed an increase of 238,000 tons in seaborne traffic and £22,819 in receipts. When he saw early in the year the prices of cotton were advancing so rapidly that spinners would practically be prohibited from importing, he was nervous as to the result. of the half year. It was perfectly clear they would have a large falling off in the receints from cotton and an early suspension of the sailing of steamers from American cotton ports. with consequent loss of other cargo. The falling off in cotton amounted to 110,000 bales, but they had had traffic to compensate for that loss, and even give an increase of 54,000 tons, which showed that they were getting an increased grip on trade, and a grip which they might depend upon it would never be relaxed. (Hear, hear.) The company had spent £2,640 more in dredging, a large portion of it being for extra dredging in the river at Warrington. As regards the Bridgewater department. there also the traffic felt the effect of the stoppage of the mills, because the Bridgewater Csnal was to a great extent dependent on bringing in cotton and taking out • cotton goods. But its results proved much better than he had expected. They had a net revenue of £11,688, as against £12.555 for the corres- ponding portion of last year. Altogether they had received .£30,193 from the Ship Canal revenue. £11,688 from the Bridgewater department, and £996 in interest, making a total half-year's profit of £92,877. Looking forward, Mr. Bythell described in detail the various developments which it is proposed to make when the necessary preliminaries have been completed for borrowing a sum of £550,000. Some work that was badly wanted was already in hand. They were putting in jetties at Mode Wheel. Irla.m, and Latchford locks, such as had been provided already at Eastham, and adding appliances to meet with increased requirements for shipping coal at Partington. Good progress had been made with the No. 9 Dock and transit shed, and he hoped that these would be ready for wprk next June. By that time there was no doubt these extensions would be constantly required, if the traffic continued to increase even in the same ratio as it had done this year. Many traffic experts, both English and foreign, had examined the new works, and these visitors were unanimous in praising their design and construc- tion. They all said there was nothing, so far as they had seen, in any other port equal to the new acccommo- dation which was being provided on the canal. That, he believed, was perfectly true. THE PROSPECTS OF THE COMPANY.

As to the prospects of the company, Mr. Bythell said there were so many influences at work that it was very difficult indeed to make any forecast about the immediate future. During the earlier months of the year timber was their greatest standby.a.nd he was glad to say that up to the present the receipts for timber were larger than last year. As to whether the advance would con- tinue it was difficult to form an opinion, and all he could say was that they lived in hope. One circumstance that embarrassed every port inEurope at present was that cargo from the United States ports and Canada continued to be relatively very scarce, particularly grain,of which scarce- ly any was being shipped from the United States ports. Many large cargo vessels had been laid up for some time, and those still running frequently came in only partly full. There own lines had been fairly well maintained, but until the Atlantic and Canadian shipping trade showed i improvement they had no prospect of extension in that direction. Nevertheless, when the new shipping season opened, about October, he looked forward to some improvement both in the matter of the quantity of cargo and in the rate of freight. The prosnects of the American cotton crop were much better than they were this time last year, aril if the good crop that was now due according to the law of averages was secured this year, there would be a considerable augmentation of Ship Canal trade. He hoped the spinners would be loyal to Manchester and snnport those shipowners who had undercalten to provide frequent sailings of large vessels to and from the :American cotton ports during the season. The Company were still dependent on spinners' impor- tations. It had not been possible, so far, to form any real cotton market in Manchester. Markets for grain and timber had been obtained, however, and no doubt cotton would follow. The question as to whether the time had not come when there should be a future market in. Manchester was being considered at the pre- sent time, and the matter would be dealt with before very lone. "We have every reason to hone," Mr. Bvthell concluded, "for a fairly good half year if not a brilliant one." (Applause.) Mr. Alderman Southern seconded the motion and it was carried.

"WAKE UP, WARRINGTON ! " Rev. C. Harvey Cook's Advice. COMMENTS ON THE MEDICAL OFFICER'S

REPORT.

"Almost an Indictment of Criminality."

In the course of his sermon at Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday morning, the Rev. C. Harvey Cook made allusion to the annual report of the Medical Officer of Health. The report, he said, ought to be in the hands of and read by every thought- ful ratepayer and housekeeper within the borough, or its salient features' printed in cheap form and distri- buted from house to house. There was a mass of in- structive information in the report, but he should only refer to one or two points. Our rates were high and they already handicapped trade and commerce. But public health was more than public wealth. A healthy borough was more than a wealthy one. One did not wish to be an alarmist, and yet one felt that this was such an important religious matter that one ought not to neglect to call attention to it. Where the town was not overcrowded or" slummy "then the death rate was practically normal; where it was over-crowded and "slummy "then the death rate was abnormal. Surely that was cause for unquiet. It seemed scarcely credible that dwelling houses only erected 20 years ago should have no damp-course in them and so have passed into a very bad state; so the report says. The unpaved streets and passages, reported on pages 108 and 109, filled men with distress, and that some of them were men- tioned 20 years ago by the then medical officer of health as needing attention yet remained unpaved to-day, was surely almost an indictment of criminality. What could be more a menace to health than pools of dirty water, sloppings from pails, fouling the surfaces of the passages or yard or street into which children would tread, carrying all sorts of things into the houses, cer- tainly conducive to illness and disease? One was forced to ask" Has not the Corporation the power when pass- ing plans for building to make it a condition that both street and passage should be properly formed and drained"? If they have the power why has it not been used, and if they have not the power why don't they get it? It was not an attractive sight to see themselves in their communal capacity in the Medical Officer of Health's report. There were a few indications of betterment, but the Prince of Wales's advice to England evidently needed repeating to War- rington in the matter of public health—" Wake up, Warrington," There was much being done, and the voluminous details in the report revealed great labour, and the churches especially should come forward and strengthen the hands of those whose duty it was to record the vital statistics of the borough. A large measure of success had attended the precautions taken against smallpox, and, as he understood Dr. Gornall, it was not that danger he feared so much now as scarlet fever. There were 231 reported cases last year, and how many unreporOd was not known. Every person in War- rington, wherever there was the slightest suspicion of its existence ought to report it. No one had the right to menace the health of another even if he or she did not care about themselves, and they of Cairo-street believed that religion ought to interest itself in these questions of health. There was no need for their minister to apologise for venturing to speak on such a matter. Life, in theirjudgment, was a whole and not a thing to be cut up into departments and labelled one "religion," another " politics," another " business," as each was but as an arc in the circle. They could not understand a reiigion that had not its vital relation to this world, not because they found in the Bible the record of care for public health, did they thereby feel bound to enjoin it upon themselves, but because experi- ence, humanity, science enforced it. They perceived through their knowing faculties that it was of necessity, necessity e93ecially to all who really believed in the Fatherhood of God and the brotherhood of man, and they could not understand a preaching of religion which was not related to the ethics of public health.

PIANO FOR DUTTON WORKHOUSE.

At the annual meeting on Wednesday the members of Runcorn Co-operative Society decided to purchase a piano for the use of the inmates of the Workhouse at Dutton.

BATHING FATALITY AT RUNCORN.

On Snnday afternoon a drowning fatality occurred in the Bridgewater Canal on the far side of Astmoor Bridge. A labourer named Arthur Cooke, living with his brother in Lyon-street, Runcorn, went with his brother to the canal to bathe. A moment or two after entering the water Cooke threw up his hands and sank. His brother went in twice but failed to bring him out. Several members of the Runcorn Grappling Corps dragged the canal without success for three or four hours. A young man named Dutton then dived into the water and re- covered the body.—The inquest was held at Runcorn on Wednesday.—Fred Cooke, brothel, said the deceased threw up his arms and sank without givinr, any warning. Samuel Dutton, of 5, Canal-street, said he dived three times and found the body in a deep hole. Cross- examined by the jury, witness stated that he had saved five lives by his swimming powers. He had also recovered two dead bodies. It was stated that Dutton had saved a life the week before. The jury returned a verdict of" Accidentally drowned," and asked that the record of Dutton should be brought under the notice of the proper authorities. _ ALI.EGED ATTEMPTED SUICIDE AT RUNCORN. _ _ _

John Fanning, a sailor, was charged at the Runcorn Police Court on Saturday, with having attempted tsa commit suicide by jumping in the Manchester Ship Canal at Runcorn on Sua.ay.—Frederick Wilkinson. 9, Waterloo-road, docks foreman, said that at half-past three on Sunday afternoon he was walking by the dock side and saw the prisoner walk to the North Pier and take-his coat and braces off. He stood on the edge of the water. Witness took him away and kept by him until he could send for the police. The man was very drunk. Two of his shipmates came along and said they, would take him to the boat. He broke away from them,' however, and staggering to the canal side, fell into the water. Witness thought he fell in by accident. He was unfit to take care of himself. He was got out in 'six minutes with a grappling iron and seemed nearly exhausted. He had not expressed to witness any inten- tion of committing suicide.—Patrick Mulligan, 4, Briscoe-street, said he saw Fanning break away from his Imates. He was very drunk and staggered into the water. He did not jump in purposely.—The Magistrates' Clerk said there was no evidence of an intention to commit suicide.—Prisoner was discharged.

ST. HELENS.

ACCIDENT TO TRADESMEN.

Two St. Helens tradesmen met with a rather serious accident on Tuesday night in one of the main streets of the town. Mr. AV. H. Aukland, of Denton's Green, was driving Mr. Thomas Middlehurst, of Hall-street, along Ormskirk-street, when the horse fell, and they were both pitched out. Mr. Middlehurst was injured on the head, and was taken to the Providence Hospital.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
ALARMING GAS EXPLOSION.

Shortly before ten -o'clock on Wednesday evening an alarming gas explosion occurred at the residence of Mr. Hefford, a baker, 23, Cowley-hill-lane, St. Helens. It appears that Mr. Hefford discovered a large escape of gas in the front sitting-room, and he procured 9. step- ladder and proceeded to fill the slide of the chandelier with water. He shouted to the servant not to come near with any light, but the girl in her excitement rushed into the room with an open light. Immediately there was a terrific explosion, the front door and bay window being hurled across the road. Mr. Hefford was enveloped in a sheet of flame, and his face and hands were badly burnt. The Fire Brigade were telephoned for, and were quickly on the scene, and speedily reduced the flames. Dr. Dow attended to Mr. Hefford's injuries. The sitting-room was wrecked, and considerable damage was done.

MURDERER'S VIEW OF LIFE.

Verses Written in a Condemned Cell.

The case of the Seaham Harbour murder, for which George Breeze paid the penalty, was remarkable for the extraordinary letters which Breeze had written. To those he added with the following effusion to a friend while under sentence of death : "Dear M.,—l thought I might have received a letter from soma of my old mates so I wrote to let you know that I am cheerful, although my position is one you would not appreciate. But when a young fellow is so easily enticed from home hv a woman he deserves to suffer. The slight quarrel had which _ _

made me leave home was through her, as she had asked me to come to lodge there. So like a fool I „ went, and then, of course, I killed her. I have a good bed and good food, and °facials are civil. Tall Smith, the football trainer, to get his men into training for the coming season. We had many a j. yful day together, especially at Blackpool. Mates, take my advice and do not let your lives be ruined. If I had taken advice I would have been with you to-day. The letter then broke into the following verse : It is hard to die so young, Especially by Billington to be hung, But when all is reckoned right Life is just a miserable sight. For in love I madly fell, And now in prison I do dwell. Ere long my neck will be bared, And for my fate I am quite prepared. So mates, don't be enticed by cheat, For life when happy is so sweet. If advice I had taken when told My name might have numbered with the old. So remember what I say, And let your life go its natural way. So now I must.bid you all adieu, For I am ready to die for her so true, As I must fill a vacant place Beside those of my shameful race. So farewell, friends, one and all, I must prepare for duty's call. The letter concluded : "ff you hear anyone say anything about the sad affair to my brother William, see that he does them no harm, as you know his temper. —Your unhappy mate, GEORGE BREEZE."

FREE BEER AT ELECTIONS.

At Leeds Assizes, on Thursday, David Snowden (40) plumber, and Isaac John Fielders Harvey (48), tobacc- onist, pleaded guilty to corruptly influencing voters at the municipal election at Barnsley last October.' Counsel stated that the practice of giving free beer at the municipal elections at Barnsley had long prevailed, and the proposal to abolish the custom by one side had not been adopted by the other. Defendants had already been subjected to very severe and serious punishment. One of them had been disfranchised, debarred from tak- ing part in municipal life for a number of years, and bad been ordered to pay something like £2,000, costs of a petition, and other costs. Justice Channel said defendants were unfortunate in being made scrapegoats, and he regretted others were not there. Taking into consideration counsel's plea for Harvey, he imposed a fine of £5O, and fined Snowden £25, defendants to pay the costs of the prosecution.
