THE EXAMINEE.

AUGUST 6th, 1904.

Monday night's debate THE CENSURE had some elbrnents of DEBATE. freshness, although the

theme was far from new. The spectacle of the Government coolly defying the opinion of the country justified Sir HENRY , CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN'S contention that Ministers are trying to undermine our fiscal system although they are afraid to appal to the electorate. They are playing a game of " manceuvres and machina- tions." They profess to be surprised by this indict- ment, and remind us over and over again that the fiscal issue is not before this Parliament, and that it belongs to a future Government. To this argu. ment Lord HUGH CECIL words a damaging answer. The Government may please themselves with this formula about the future, but the country persists in regarding the issue as immediate. Every bye- election proves that to the hilt. By common agreement, Oswestry was a pitched battle on the whole Protectionist policy. What is the use of pretending that the present Parliament has nothing to do with the matter when Oswestry concerns itself with the matter and sends to the House of Commons a representative pledged to resist Mr. CHAMBERLAIN to the last ? The force of this argument was not lost on the Apostle of Tariff Reform. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN knows what Oswestry means, and he sought to soften the edge of it for the benefit of the Government. He differed from Mr. BA.LPOUR, he said, upon the main issue. Why had he left the Cabinet? The PRIME MINISTER SYMPStilified with the object of his policy, but did not believe that proferentia 1 tariffs would commend themselves to the country.

Mr. CHAMBERLAIN reiterated hit, own belief in his ultimate triumph, but meanwhile he would never accept the theory that Sheffield meant Birming- ham. It was something quite different, something that the Unionist Free Traders had no occasion to shy at. This, of course,was intended to meet Lord Hcaff CECIL'S warning, that if Mr. BALFOUR indicated any readiness to move aa inch beyond the Sheffield progeamme, the Free Fooders would go into the division lobby against him. So the Birmingham Damon deplored his failure to persuade the Sheffield Phthias to make common cause with him. A perfectly unreal difference of opinion between them was set up solely for the purpose of Monday night's division. It was another illus- tration of the policy of machination and manceuvre. Here are the constituencies declaring with growing emphasis that they want neither Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN nor Mr. BALFOUR, and here is Mr. CHAM- BERLAIN giving Mr. BALFOUR a certificate Of competence to represent public opinion at the head of affairs. It is a ludicrous situation, which would not be maintained at the fag end of a most unsatisfactory Session if the Unionist Free !Traders had the courage of their convictions. Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN dwelt none too strongly upon the indifference of Mr. BALFOUR and his colleagues to the collective responsibility of the Cabinet. The Albert Hall demonstration is not the only instance. It is notorious that Mr. ARNOLD-FORSTER'S Army scheme does not repre- sent the judgment of " the Government or a Government." It has been openly repudiated by Mr. BRODRICK and Lord LANsnowNE,and the War Secretary, who does not adapt himself readily to the policy of manoeuvres, is meditatang retire- ment in disgust. There are cases on record, no doubt, in which members of a Cabinet have differed from one another on particular issues, but not upon issues which demanded immediate appeal to the country. Such differences of opinion related to matters which at the time were merely subsidiary, and not to matters which engaged the passionate interest of the whole nation. Mr. BALFOUR has established the novel precedent of a Prime Minister who holds that he and his colleagues may say and do what eath individual pleases about a question on which they are beaten by the electors whenever the electors have the opportunity of declaring their minds. If this is not unconstitutional, what in the name of wonder does 3onstitutional mean Even Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN hinted that the farce ought to come to an end in a General Election.

The Town Council meeting to be held on Tues- day evening promises to be a protracted one, for there are a number of subjects on the agenda which are likely to lead to discussion. In the first place, the Mayor announces the receipt of the report on the inquiry from the three Commis- sioners appointed by the Corporation. If a discussion cornmenees on the vexed question of the inquiry at the present juncture there is no telling where and how it will end. Under the circumstances it would appear to be wise to postpone any discussion until the Com- inissioners appointed by the Non-Trading Cim- mittee have finished their investigations and furnished the Council with their report. The resolution of the Finance Committee recommend- ing the acceptance of the tender of Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, Limited, for the parchase of the licence of the George Hotel at £2,550 will certainly provoke some discussion. The result of the last big barter with the brewers which resulted in the transference of the licence of the Green Dragon Inn to the Brooklands is still fresh in the memory of the public. Is another fair district of the town to be pxploited by the brewers, simply because the George Inn has to be ciemolished ? It appears to us that the Finance and Estate Committee would have acted in toe best interests of the community if they had allowed the licence to lapse. £2,550 is certainly a tempting bait, but who is going to profit in the long run? We hope the members of the Corpora- tion will have the courage to follow the exarn.ole oil eading municipalities in the kingdoin and let the licence lapse.

The mere fact that the annual report of our Medical Officer of Health has been discussed in a place of worship, although unusual, is in itself a healthy sign. A matter which so closely concerns the health of the community is not outside the range of even a pulpit, and we can never hope to have a healthy town until the townspeople are roused from their apathy on this most important subject, and led to take such an interest in hygiene that they will be determined to have the serious sanitary defects of the town remedied. The Rev. C. Harvey Cook in the course of his reference to the suUject at Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday, advised the adoption of the motto—" Wake up Warrington!" A great deal of work needs to ba done before we can c9ngratulate ourselves on the completeness and:efficiency of our sanitary arrange- ments. It is of little use for the Corporation to spend time in widen'ng main thoroughfares or embark- ing on large commercial undertakings if such elementary work of local self-government as the paving of streets and back passages, and the i purifying of slum areas s i only partially or perfunctorily attended to. This s the most pres- sing work to which the Corporation can direct their attention, and until it is attended to, we shall be called upon to deal with epidemics and be faced with mortality returns of an unfavourable character.

Mr. W. S. Haddock, the manager of the gas works, has a very good account to give of the past year's working of this Corporation department. A profit of over £lO,OOO has been made—the largest realised in the history of the undertaking. The Gas Committee has since made several concessions to consumers, and these are calculated to reduce the income by £1,500. Mr. Haddock however,is hopeful-that this sum may be recovered through a more extended use ot gas. The conces- sions have been made to consumers outside the Borough who now get their gas 31, per thousand feet cheaper, and smaller concessions have been made to users of gas engines and consumers by slot meter. One may naturally ask why has the ordin- ary consumer in the Borough b3en passed over? Surely with a profit running into five figures the gas committee can afford to be generous to their lbest customers! Even with the discount knocked off, the price of gas to the ordinary consumer in the Borough is 2s. 7d. per thousand cubic feet. This is a higher price than is demanded ia neighnouring towns and the present seems n favourable opportunity to ask tor a reduction. A concession of even a Donny per thousand feet of gas would be greatly appreciated by consumers in the borough, who at the present time, are feeling the burden of the heavy rates.

Paculiarly saddenipg are the circumstances of the double drowning fatality which we record to-day. Yet there stands out an example of true heroism which must awaken a feeling of pride in the mind of the reader. The cry for help was unhesitatingly answered, and a boy of sixteen sac- rificed life itself in the endeavour to save his friend. Truly a noble end to a young career! It is ptinful to reflect that the summer season, when so many people give themselves up to enjoy- ment, always claims its toll of deaths. Nothing could be mote natural than that boys should seek relief from the oppressive heat in a cooling dip. The pity of it is that many cannot or will not avail themselves of the facilities provided by the Corporation, and inexpert swimmers expose them- selves to gmve dangers by bathing in deep water- ways. T.) such we trust this distressing fatality will act as a timely waraing.

The weird and uncouth sound of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sans fire alarm disturbed the Sabbath quietude of Warrington on Sunday evening last. The noise could be heard for miles around, and although it was t. 311 o'clock at night, thousands of people turned out of their homes and hastened to the firm's factory,believing that a seriols fire had broken out there. The firm's brigade with its nsual smartness was the first on the scene and a number of head officials arrived imme- diately after. The thoroughfares surrlunding the works became almost impassible owing to the press of the people, but it speedily leaked out that no fire had broken out. It seemed to be another illustration of the cry " Wolf ! Wolf !” when there was no wolf. On hearing that there was no cause for the fire alarm, some of the beguiled ones became sarcastic, whilst others used expressions more forcible than polite. In this way the firm and its fire brigade were held up to a great deal of unmerited and totally unwarrantable ridi- cule. The truth is that they were the victims of a malicious and mischievous prank on the part of one of their own workmen, who in a mad, freakish moment sounded the alarm. He was summarily dealt with for causing the scare, and it is to be hoped that his punishment will prevent a recur- rence of an incident whi?.h the firm regrets ever took place.

Toe threat of the Warrington Corporation to take 'proceedings to close the market which is held several times a week in Vict 3ria Squire, Stockton Heath has acted like a bombshell in the village. In certain quarters where men generally congregate it has been the main topic of conversation, and some very strong remarks have been made by some of them as to the foplish and ruinous policy adopted by the village leaders during the past few months. It is coming to be recognized that the position of Stockton Heath is not that of an independent community, but dependent upon the good graces of Warrington, and that she would he wise in future to adopt the" open door" policy when Warrington makes her any proposals.

Melancholy news comes from Port Arthur, relieved, perhaps, by the story of the gallantry of defence and attack. Bat the loss of life is shock- ing. While it may be hoped that the worst is now over, what has happened only indicates what has still to come. The Jap mese are not this time finding Port Arthur a nut between the crackers; they cannot walk into it as they did wnen the Chinese held it a few years ego. Every mile of the advance, every fort, mast be dear-bought, and for the present the besieged, despite the inferno in which they live, have flung off the assault. Every pane of glass in the town is broken, every hospital is "swamped," and after a momentary view of horrors in town and hills beyond the curtain again falls on the fate of the distressed town and the plans of toe grim enemy sitting at its gates. The fell of the port must be only a matter of time, and the Japanese will regard the appar- ent suc2ess of the garrison as maskincr the demora- lised condition which such a bombardment and siege together are calculated to bring about. With the signal success of the Japanese against Kuro- patkin's position, it would be a dramatic climax to a war of dramatio surprises if crowning and de- cisive successes to Japan's arms were to occur almost simultaneously to the north and south of the peninsula. The completeness of their methods is vvell known, and their difficulties at the pre=ent hour impart an added zest • to the chances o: the situation.

Sir Harry Poland's letter in the " Times" on Thursday,exhibits one of the worst defects of our Parliamentary system. In the concentration on a few measures which excite party feeling, matters of great importance are ne.4lecte3, although th.re is substantial agreement as to their need; ar.d in that sense they are non-controversial. The False Statements (Companies) Bill was the natural sequel to the Whitaker Wright trial. Tile Attorney-General, with the characteristic slowness of a lawyer to move, had to be subjected to con- siderable pressure; but at last he introducal the measure, which is shore, clear, and to the point. It applies to a person who pub- lishes " any written statement or account relating to the financial affairs of a company which he knows to be false in any material particular " ; but it only operates in regard to false statements in documents already requirei by Act of Parlia- ment. Not a word has been said against it, and it would protect not only the ordinary investor, nut the poorer classes, by the demand which it would make for greater truthfulness in regard to industrial societies. If progress with this sort of measure cannot be made in intervals of heavier work, it is time that the machinery should be revised, and there should be delegation to respon- sible commitiees of at least the Committee stage of much more of the business of the House.

Everybody is watching with interest the develop_ rnent of the great strike in the American meat trade. All the Protectionist journals mention the fact that a prolonged dislocation of the American supplies will cause a meat famine in this country. Exactly so! But where are the supplies from the Colonies which these very journals have never ceased to inform us are sufficient for our needs if only we could keep the American supplies out by an adequate protective taxation? As a matter of fact, the Colonial supplies have this year fallen off seriously. Of mutton from New Z3aland we have had in the first six months of 1904 only 988,700c.,wt, as against 1,258,048 cwt. in the corresponding period of 1903. Canadian cattle imports have also fallen from 68,000 to 53,000. Even Anstraban rabbits Nave declined from 108,0003 wt to 34,000 .:wt. Evidently, if we are to depend on Colonial sup- plies, the risk of a meat famine is very serious.

How little Canada stands in need of atly arti- ficial stimulus such as the 2s.preference on wheat, is shown by the enormous increase in her wheat crop for 1904. Last year the crop was 55,000,000 bushels, and in 1902 a rec•ird yield was attained with 68,009,900 bushels. But the lowest estimate for the current year is 77,090,000 bushels. Obvi- ously, the natural rate of expansion is greater than any which could be brought about by a small pre- ference in favour of Colonial-grown grain. With- out any interference on the part of the Mother Country, it is safe to predict that Canada must become before maily decades the greatest wheat-exporting, if not the greatest wheat-producing country in the world. The latest move in the Canadian tariff adjustment tends, like the other change, to handicap British trade with the Dominion. Originally the Govern- ment proposed to levy a duty on British window glass of 7i per cent., while leaving the duty on foreign glass at 20 per cent. It appeared, however, that the production of window glass in great Britain is virtually a monopoly of a St. Helens firm, so that the Canadian Government, rather than benefit a single firm, has decided to reduce the duty on foreign glass to 15 per cent. Under these circumstances the American window glass exporters should be in a position to keep the Canadian market without much difficulty. Last year British glass and glass wares only accounted for £91,000 out of the £410,000 worth of foreign glass imported into Canada, and the new duties hardly promise us a larger share.

The Living of Ashton Hayes. The living of A.shton Hayes, near Chester, which is in the gift of Mr. Charlton Parr, has been offered to and accepted by the Right Rev. Charles Frederick Douet, D.D., assistant bishop of Jamaica. Dr. Douet has been rector of Mandeville, diocese of Jamaica,since 1892. The living of Ashton Hayes has income of £250 gross, and Mil net., with residence.

Scene In a Church. A. singular incident occurred in St. Alban's Roman Catholic Church, Bewsey-street, an Monday evening. A man who resides in Pierpoint-street, and is evidently suffering from religious mania, was found in the building in a very excited condition. He kept calling out" The voice, the yoke !" Police officers were sent for, and they experienced some difficulty in getting him in a cab. Eventually he was removed to the Workhouse for his mental state to be inquired into.

Fire in Parker-street. Shortly after one o'clock on Sunday morning, a fire broke out on the premises of Mr. G.. W. Johnson baker, 36, Parker-street. The fire bell was rung and the superintendent and deputy superintendent, with a good number of ma, at once turned out with the steam engine Major. Alter about an hour's work the flames were extinguished, the damage, which is covered by insurance, being comparatively small. The origin of the fire is unknown. Coddington paired for the Government and Mr. Shackleton and Mr. Parrott against. Extension of the Tramways. The work of laying the new tram lines along Old London Road is being proceeded with rapidly, the first pair of lines having already reached as far as • Victoria-square. The railings of the hous,_.s opposite the Church wall and along the road have during the week been pulled down by men in the employ of the Corporation in order to widen the road to the adequate width of 45 feet, as arranged between the Cheshire County Council, to whom the road belongs, and the Warrington Corporation. Three Warrington • young gentlemen had been for a drive to Farnworth and Hale, and were :being taken to their respective homes, when the horse bolted from Heathside along Scotland-road, Mersey-street, and past the Cannons into Bold-street. Here the occupants were thrown out, and Mr. James Prinsep, of Lovely-lane, sustained a nasty scalp wound which rendered him unconscious. Mr. Sydney Griffiths, of Swansea (late of Warrington) was also hurt, his ankle being dislocated. Dr. R. 'Smith attended the sufferers, who were afterwards able to proceed home. ' School Children's Treat. The school children of Croft had their annual treat on Saturday. The weather was delightful, and long before the time appointed,numbers of the children had arrived at the schools. About 2 p.m. the Liverpool • (Newton) Farm Band, conducted by Mr. Smith,arrived, and the procession immediately started, the route being from the school to Smithy-brow, thence up Heath-lane, back to Littletown, afterwards to school. A band of willing helpers had in the interval been preparing the tea, and after this had been disposed • of,an adjournment was made to afield which had been kindly, lent by Mr. W. Sankey, where races 41-. c., for money prizes were competed for by the scholars. The 'band played selections for dancing. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFT TONIC is the KING or TONICS. 6.3. SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. 'Another Outbreak of Smallpox. Warrington has unfortunately experienced another outbreak of smallpox. Last week six cases were reported and three others have been added this week. Two patients have died. The Public Health Depart- ment has had some trouble in tracing one of the contacts. but yesterday morning they succeeded in finding the object of their quest at Warrington Bridge. The man had been into Cheshire looking for work and had thus evaded the authorities. He was found to be suffering from a mild attack of smallpox, and was immediately taken to the Town Hall stables and detained there, pending the arrival of the cab used for the removal of smallpox patients. 'Fire in Lovely-lane. Shortly after one o'clock on Wednesday afternoon, the Warrington Fire Brigade was called to a fire at the stables of Qouncillor Walter Brough in Lovely- -lane. Superintendent Turner and a good muster of men were quickly on the scene with the engine "Major." It was found that some hay in the loft had. 'become ignited, but fortunately the flames were extin- guished without much difficulty. Damage to the extent of about 110 was done and this is unfortun- ately not covered by insurance. Messrs. Crosfield's fire *brigade turned out, but on finding that the Borough brigade had been summoned, returned to the works. The origin of the fire isnot known. Music in the Park. Mr. T. J. Peterson) ; eeleetion 2nd, "Bohemian girl," Balfe ; "God save The King." Financial Suicide " of Lancash ire. At a.meeting of the Lancashire County Council on Thursday. Mr. Garnett, of Lancaster, said though large amounts must necessarily be spent unnn police, main roads, an'cl education, a considerable saving could be effected in other directions. There were con- tinual demands for increased salaries, and a large sum could have been saved in the erection of a new sessions house. Money had been lavishly spent upon Police stations, and unless there was retrenchment, it would spell financial suicide. The financial area was decreasing. but no effort was made to reduce the ex- Penditure, indeed the prospective expenditure was greater. The gradual increase in rateable value did not arise from the erection of new buildings -but through old buildings and property being rated from twentv to twenty-five per cent. higher than formerly. The ratepayers were becoming dissatisfied, urban districts were petitioning to join county boroughs, and non-county boroughs were endeavour- ing to amalgamate. Where was the county going to be landed? The minty area was being depleted, and unless the Council dealt with the matter in a dis- tlassionate manner they would soon be faced with financial ruin. The ratepayers were being ton heavily *burdened and becoming alarmed. After a discussion, Mr. Garnett gave notice that at the next quarterly Meeting of the County Council be would move that a trong committee be anneinted to fully investigate the PnanCial condition of the county.

Liberal Voters Please Note ! The new list of voters for 1901 is now published and affixed to the door of every place of worship in the Borough, and can also be seen at the Liberal Club, Bewsey-street. In view of the fact that the next general election will in all probability be fought on this register, it is incumbent upon every Liberal to see that his name is on the list. Every Liberal who has been a householder for 12 months ending July 15th, last, should examine the list for himself, and if his name is omitted, or if there is any error or mis- description of any kind whatever, he should inform Mr. Clayton, the Liberal agent, at once, so that the vote may not be lost. Lodgers who occupy one or more rooms of the clear value of :£lO are also entitled to vote, and should claim immediately. Claim forms and all assistance will be readily furnished on applica- tion at the Registration offices, Liberal Club, War- rington. All claims must be served upon the overseers onor before August 20th. LADIES BEST GLACE BOOTS and SHOES, UNDER the CLOCK, BR.ID GE-STREET.—AchI. Homing Pigeons and Farm Crops. At the Mold Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, before Major Philips (presiding) and Mr. E. Wheldon, Samuel White, a working man, of Buckley, summoned William Higgins, a farmer on a large scale, also of Buckley, for wounding and killing pigeons. Mr. H. J. Longton, solicitor, Warrington, appeared for the complainant, and Mr. W. H. Churton, solicitor, of Chester, defended. —Complainant alleged that on the oth July the defend- ant shot at his pigeons whilst the latter were on the wing, with the result that one pigeon was killed, and two others were wounded. One of the wounded birds had, however, died since the issue of the summons. For the defence it was contended that there was o large number of persons in the neighbourhood wha kept homing pigeons, which did much mischief to the crops. He fired at the birds when they were on the crops.--Tlte Chairman said that a farmer had a right to ,shoot any pigeons that were injuring his crops. The summons would be dismissed.

UNDER the CLOCK for GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. Next Thursday's Show at Walto3 Park. The Warrington and District Agricultural Show will be held next Thursday at. Walton Hall, the resi- dence of Sir Gilbert Greenall,Bart. There is a splendid entry all through, a record entry, in fact. Shires are, as usual, of great excellence. The district hackney classes are always well filled. All the best hunters, winners at Peterboro', Royal Lancashire, 874., will appear, while any local exhibitor who has entered and wishes to sell his animal is sure to find a ready customer for a saleable horse, trade having hitherto been very brisk at previous shows. The harness classes should prove very interesting, as being so near the end of the showing season decisions at this show are often considered as the final court of appeal. Jumpers are a special feature, and it is only neces- sary to say that Mr. Taylor, of Ormskirk (who has had the honour of being presented to his Majesty) has entered to say there will be no lack of interest and excitement. The Yorkshire Hussars' band will play all the afternoon. There are special arrangements in case of rain. Plank roads have been laid, footboards placed round the ring, and in addition to the covered grand stand a large shelter has been erected. The gardens, greenhouses, etc., at Walton Hall, will be thrown open to the public, the herd of prize pigs and Kerry cattle will also be on view, while horse lovers can also inspect about 103 pedigree hackneys and ponies from Sir Gilberes stud, which will be sold without reserve on the day following the show.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIE% TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63. SANKEY-STRE ST, Warrington.—Advt. The Late Mrs. Birtles. At Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday evening, the Rev. Jlha Yong e m:tde a touching re- ference to the late Mrs. Thomas Birtles, who was for many years associated with the church. At the close of his diacourse on the words "Blessed are the dead who die in the Lord! Yea saith the Spirit, for they rest from their labour and their works do follow them," Mr. Yonge said he hoped the message might carry some help to those present, who by the death, of Mrs. Birtles, were under the sad shadow of a great loss. To have the happy wedded life of 46 years terminated, to realise that the motherhboct that had been so true and beautiful and rich in sympathy was ceased—these were among the greatest sorrows that could be felt. The loyal, faithful wife; the loving watchful mother; the unselfish sympathetic, devoted friend had "died in the Lord," whom she loved with simple, quiet, unobtrusive, constant devotion. She rested now from the labours of a busy life by which she ministered to those dear to her with scarcely ever a thought for herself By a most general and cordial testimony it was certi- fied that her life afforded beautiful example of what wifehood and motherhood should be. And it could not therefore be other than that those in whose midst her life had been spent should intensely, sorrow in her loss. But after all, their experi- ence was not all loss. They had a memory that must remain with them as a living and powerful I and beautiful influence. Let them not think she were dead. That word, and the thought it carried, should be deleted from our Christian speech. We did not know, of course, .whether our departed ones were far from us or near, but an inspired writer had told us that we pass our life surrounded by "a great cloud of witnesses." Surely it could not be unreasonable to surmise that in that great onlooking company would be such as had cared for us most in this lower life. Hence though the departed were out of our sight, we might be ever in theirs. This ought to be a great spur to fidelity.

BANK UOLIDAY.

The Week-end Exodus from Warrington.

- - Holiday-makers revelled in delightful sunshine last week-end, and Warrington contributed its quota to swell the throngs at the popular resorts. The railway facilities were largely taken advantage of, and there were scenes of mutilation at the stations on Saturday and Bank Holiday. The bookings were rather heavier than usual, as the returns from both companies show. By the London and North-Western Railway GOO journeyed to Blackpool during the week-end, and 100 to Morecambe. There were about 60 passengers to various parts of Scotland and over 150 to North Wales. On Monday 130 booked to Llangollen: 200 to Chester; 100 to Colwyn Bay; 80 to Holvwell ; 80 to Windermere; 30 to Southport; 600 to Frodsham ; and 120 to Helsby. At the Central Station the book- ings during the week-end were :—Liverpool, 1,000; New Brighton, 1,000 Southport, 1,o00; Douglas, 130 ; Llandudno, 100; Belle Vue and Manchester, 1,400. For those who remained at home on Monday, the chief attraction was the athletic festival promoted by the Football Club. Cyclists were able to enjoy themselves to the full, and picturesque Cheshire was the favourite resort.

WORKS TRIP TO BLACKPOOL. The employes of the St. Helens Cable Company had a successful trip to Blaanool on Saturday. The directors had arranged for cheap fares,and had secured the holders the privilege of free admission to the. Tower and one of the piers. About 500 commenced the journey at an early hour on Saturday morning and arrived at Blackpool shortly after seven o'clock. Throughout the day thA weather was glorious, and the holiday makers enjoyed themselves to the full. The return Journey was com- menced at nine o'clock, Warrington being safely reached about eleven. Some of the visitors availed themselves of the extension tickets which enabled them to spend the week-end at the favourite resort. The arrangements were carried out by Mr. C. Manning. hon. treasurer ; Mr. F. Webster. hon. secretary,and others from various departments. It is proposed to make the excursion an annual one.

PORTER'S NARROW ESCAPE.

- -- During the stress of the Bank Holiday traffic at the Central Station on Monday morning, a warehouseman named William Byteld, who residAs in Padgate-lane, had a very narrow escape. He was acting as temporary porter, and was closing the door of a compartment whilst a train was in motion when he fell between the train and the platform. Railway Constable Humphries. who was standing near at the time, extricated Byfield whose arms and back had been rather badly bruised. He was conveyed to the Infirmary in a cab and his injuries were attended to, after which he was allowed to leave.

TOWN COUNCIL BUSINESS.

Subjects for Consideration at Tuesday's Meeting.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Coun- cil will be held at the Town Hall at six o'clock on Tuesday evening. The following among other business will be considered:—

THE GEORGE HOTEL LICENCE.

The Finance Committee having invited tenders for the purchase of the licence of the George Hotel, con- ditionally upon the magistrates consenting to its trans- fer to other premises. The following tenders were received :---Mr, Thomas Halton, £GSO ; Mr. Benjamin Kenyep, £1,000; Mr. William Bolton, £1,500; Messrs. P. Walker and Sons, Limited, £2,550; It was resolved that the tender of Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, Limited, for the purchase of the licence subject to the conditions named in their offer, at £2,550 be accepted; also that on the licence being transferred, the premises of the George Hotel be demolished, the site being required for the rebuilding of premises acquired for the widening of Bridge-street.

LAB ITR A.TION AN ARDS,-M ARK ET- GATE( IM PROVEM ENTS.

The Town Clerk reported that he had received the award of Mr. William Owen in the arbitration between Mr. J. C. Sutton and the Corporation with regard to the purchase of four shops at the corner of Knutsford- road and Wilderspool-causeway, ten honses in Pinuers- brow, numbered 10 to 14, 22 to 26, and 30 to 36, a plot f laud contlining 2,0[7 3-9 squire yards, with the two houses in Winwick-road, Nos. 31 to 33, standing thereon, and that the arbitrator had awarded Mr. Sutton the sum of £4,750. The Town Clerk also reported that he had received the award of Mr. Owen in the arbitration between Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd., and the Corporption with regard to the purchase of a plot of land at the corner of Howley-lane and Church-street, containing about 1,200 square yards, and that the arbitrator had awarded Messrs. Rylands Bros., the sum of £2,670. It was resolved that the Finance and Estate Com- mittee be recommended to pull down the premises in Bridge-street near Market-gate to enable new buildings to be erected.

PROPOSED EXTENSION AT THE SANATORIUM.

At a meeting of the Health Committee it was resolved that Miss Stott be appointed an inspector of nuisances at 20s. per week, the engagement to be subject to 14 days' notice to be given on either side; that the Borough Surveyor be instructed to prepare plans for the erection of an administrative block at the sanatorium and that the medical officer of health be instructed to consult with the Borough Surveyor as to the details; that the Chairman and Mr. Councillor Wilkinson, with the Medical Officer of Health be appointed to meet the Mayor (as chairman of the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee) with reference to the provision of office ac3ommodation for the whole of the staff of the health department at Bank House offices; that the tender of Mr. H. H. Dell for painting and colouring required at the infectious diseases hospital according to specifica- tion at £4O be accepted.

MORE ABUT TRE PAILS AND ASEIBINS CONTRACT. SANKEY BROOK.

The Sanitary Works Committee considered a letter from Mr. Stanley Mobberley,solicitor, Lye,Stourbridge, who wrote on behalf of Mesrs. Rhodes and Sons, with reference to reports of the April and May meetings of the Town Council. The writer stated: As the statement made by Mr. Stott was absolutely void of foundation, and as the circulation thereof has already injured my clients' business and is calculated to cause them further loss unless repudiated, my. clients desire me to respectfully ask your Council to pass a resolution to the effect that after personal enquiry and investigation by your members' you were satisfied that the allegation of not paying the recognized rate of wages in the trade was made upon reliable informqtion. I trust your Council will kindly assist my clients in this matter in the way indicated. It was resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to reply to the letter stating that in view of the fact that the Council had passed a resolution accepting their tender for the supply of pails and ashbins was i* itself sufficient to prove that they believed that the firm paid thP recognized rate of wages in the district. It was resolved that the refuse from the Markets be destroyed at the dept as heretofore, provided the Markets Committee are agreeable to pay the actual cost. _ _ _ _ _ _

Mr. Ruddock attended before the Committee, and reported fully upon the samples of water taken from Sankey Brook, which he had treated on a temporary bacteria bed with very satisfactory results. After dis- cussion the further consideration of the matter was de- ferred.

ERNEST KELLY.".

REPORT BY THE BOROUGH TREASURER.

The Position of the Police Superannuation

Fund.

The Borough Treasurer (Nlr. J. Fairhurst) recently attended the annual meeting of the Institute of Treasurers and Accountants held at Harrogate and at a meeting of the Finance and Estate Committee he pre- sented an interesting report in which he shows our position locally, in relation to the principal subjects dis- cussed. These were consolidation of rating, the Educa- tion Act of 1902, Police Superannuation Fund, and the Valuation Bill 1901.

CONSOLIDATION OF RATING.-WHAT WARRINGTON HAS

GAINED

This matter had been before the executive for con- sideration, and they advise that an effort should be made to bring about a reform in the collection of rates. They recommend that there should be one consolidated rate for all purposes, to be levied and collected by Borough Councils, as being the most direct, economical and convenient to ratepayers and Corporations alike. I may say that part of the scheme has already been carried out in Warrington, as in 1899 the collection of the Poor Rate was transferred from the Guardians to the Cor- poration, the present annual saving_ _being about £7OO.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
ED CICATION ACT. 19C2.

A very able paper was read by .the Borough Treasurer of Accrington on Elementary Education in relation to local taxation and finance, and a useful comparison made as to how the Act hai affected thi different towns, the increase in the rates varying this year from 3d. to Is. 3d. in the £.

The unenviable position in which education authori• ties (where there had not previously been a School Board) found themselves on the "appointed day" owing to the absence of funds to finance voluntary schools, has been somewhat relieved by the operation of the Educa- tion (Provision of Working Balances) Act. 1903, under which education authorities were enabled, with the sanction of the Local Government Board. to provide either by loan or by overdraft, a working balance for carrying the Act into effect.

POLICE SUPERANNUATION FUNDS.-. SERIOUS POSITION.

A paper was also. read on Police Superannuation Funds by the Borough Treasurer of Blackburn, who pointed out the serious state of some of the Funds. In our case the balance of grant, after deducting a sum equal to amount contributed by members of the force, was in 1892 94.87 per cent. of amount paid out of fund; In 1904, 11.46 per cent. Our income for the year ended 31st March, 1904, was £946 4s. 11d., and the expenditure £222 ss. id., and I may point out that no part of the capital invested can be used for the purpose of paying pensions, I have ascertained from the Chief Constable that in th„ next five years eleven members of the force will be entitled to pensions. Their present salaries amouunt to £1,503 per annum, and if the services be approved and continuous, they would receive, say, £l,OOO a year in pensions; this adeed to our present pension list would be a serious matter, as we should then have to call upon the rates to pay the difference. It was stated at the meeting that many persons under 50 years of age were recsiving pensions who were well able to fulfil the duties of constable for several years longer. In our own case it is possible for a constable to retire at 464 years of age. In view of its seriousness, it may be thought advisable to give this matter consideration by a joint sub-com- mittee of the Finance and Watch Committees. VALUATION BILL, 1904.—1 TS EFFECT ON WARRINGTON.

The Bill provides for the transference of the con- stitution of Assessment Committees from the Guardians to County and County Borough Councils, and the allowances of fixed deductions for ratable value. The deductions are much in excess of what are at present allowed in Warrington,and if the Bill becomes law it will alter the local incidence of rating cansiderably.The ratable value of Warrington, I estimate, will be reduced 4 per cent. and the rate would have to be increased proportionately to meet this deduction • and as our borrowing powers are based upon our ratable value, this would be reduced by say, about £20,000. There seems, however, no chance of the Bill becoming law this Session.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON.

ENDING JULY 30th, 190 i.

During the week, the following births and deaths were registered :—Warrington Registration District births,743 ; deaths, 23. The excess of births over deaths was 20.

The death-rate from all causes was equal to 17.5 per 1,000, against 13.1 the average of the previous 4 weeks and against 25'5 the corresponding period of tast year. The death-rate from the seven chief zyrnotic diseases was equal to 3.04 per 1,000, against 2.4 the average rate of the previous 4 weeks and against 3.0 the correspond- ing period of 1903. The ages of mortality were :- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 6 1 4 6 23

The deaths included-1 from whooping cough, 2 from diarrhcea, 1 from erysipelas, 2 from other tubercular diseases, 3 from cancer, 1 from bronchitis, 2 from heart diseases, 11 from all other causes.

The fo' llowing cases of infectious disease have been reported during Om wee :—Small-pox, 6 cases, scarlet fever, 10 cases. Total number of patients in hospital, week ending July 30th, 75. _

NiErEOR. OLOGY.—The mean temperture was 63 Fah., and therefore 1 deg. lower than the preceding week. The maximum temperature 78 F., was recorded on the 24th inst., and the minimum temperature 52 F., on the 26th inst.

The rainfall measured '69 inch. The general direction was S.W.

.T. GUEST GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

GRAPPENHALL

THE CRICKET CLUB.—The Ist eleven were at home to Ptinketh and gave the latter a fair amount of leather- hunting. The total of the visitors only reached 29, while Grappenhall topped the century by three runs. Warburton put together the- highest score for the winners with 36, Banks, Gifford and England following with 15, 14 and 10 respectively. Warburton and Watson bowled very effectually, the former being credited with 5 wickets at a cost of 15 runs. whilst Watson was more successful still with 5 for 7. The 2nd eleven sustained a crushing defeat at the hands of AltrinchamSt.Margarets. Bank Holiday! Whit Monday and August Bank Holidays are set apart for fixtures with Ashton Heyes, and on Monday the return match was played at Grappenhall in glorious weather. Being an all day match, play began about eleven o'clock,the visitors electing to bat. The batsmen made rather merry with the bowling, 110 being recorded Wore the tenth wicket fell. The home Players fell considerably short of their opp?nent's figures, 70 only being recorded. The second innings produced a reversal of figures, however, the visitors Iscoring 71 to Grappenhall's 115, thus leaving the homesters victorious by the narrow margin of four runs. For the losers Shallcross compiled 35 (not ont), Hayes 130, Mounfield 13. Schofield 23, and T. Carter 12. Moun- Ifield and Schofield bore the brunt of the bowling in both innings. On the winning side the chief scores were Carter 33, Banks 22, Kaye 21, Pearson 16, Warburton 15 and Ellis 9. Warburton claimed 7 wickets for 40 runs, Carter 4 forlB and Watson 4 for 8. By the kindness of Mr. A. L. Kaye dinner and tea were provided for both !elevens.

Lieutenant-Colonel the Hon. Evelyn Barring, 10th • Hussars, has been re-appointed military secretary to Lord Curzon, and will accompany his lordship on his return to India. THE INQUIRY REPORT. MORE CORRESPONDENCE. The Non-Trading Commissioners Write to the Mayor. Further Examination of Books Necessary. The following is a copy of a letter which has' been sent to the Mayor by the Commissioners appointed by the Non-Trading Committee: Warrington, sth Augnst, 1901. TO HIS WORSHIP THE MAYOR OF WARRINGTON. Dear Sir,

TRADING INQUIRY

At their sitting on Thursday, 21st July, the Commis- sioners, of whom only five were present, resolved, by a vote of three against two, to bring the inquiry to an end.

The undersigned three Commissioners beg to pro- test very strongly against this decision. With a view to elucidate a number of cases up ia which evidence has already been taken, they find it. absolutely neces- sary and indispensable to make an examination of a number of books and papers which belong to the Corporation, and to ask from the officials, if they find it necessary, an explanation of figures and facts arising during such examination. They therefore desire that your Council will give instructions that the Town Clerk shall, as heretofore, place at our disp3sal, on such days as may be arranged, such documents or books as we may desire to inspect, and that we shall be authorized to call upon such of your officials as we may think necessary, to give explanations which the circumstances may seem to require. We had practically agreed with our three ellleagues that the time had arrived when we should not call further outside witnesses, but we have always intended to complete the investigation mentioned in this letter.

We ask y3u therefore, in the hope of satisfactorily concluding the Inquiry, to afford us the facilities already mentioned, without which it is our opinion that a great part of the time already spent will be entirely thrown away, and the result arrived at will be so incomplete as to cause serious dissatisfaction on the part of the burgesses who will be called upon to pay the expenses of the Inquiry. As no shorthand notes will be necessary, our further investigation will not entail any additional outlay of the public moneys. We are, Dear Sir, Yours very respectfully, • S. NORBURY WILLIAMS, JOHN PEMBERTON, ADAM HORROCKS.

Mr. Birchall Replies to Mr. Norbury Williams.

TO MR EDITOR OF "THE EXAMINER."

Sir,—Notwithstanding the mild assumption of righteous indignation by Mr. Norbury Williams in his letter on Saturday last, I honestly believe that both he and his colleagues are rather glad that the inquiry has been closed. Two months ago we were all practically agreed that the report should be cotn- menced, yet, since then they have been dribbling the meetings,on though literally doing nothing to the purpose. At our last meeting they had nothing to propose. Mr. Williams was away and Mr. Horrocks coolly informed us that he was going away for a fortnight, though he had already been absent from about one-fourth of our previous meetings, and when we considered that this indefinite prolongation of the proceedings, with so little result was costing the rate- payers from ten to fifteen pounds per week, I think we rightly concluded it was time to stop. hr. Wi lliams'ne w- born desire to again consult Corporation documents, is a surprise to me as for weeks past he has not hinted at such a wish. As he has had II months during which every book or document we could think of, as bearing on the i aqui ry,has been asked for awl prouantiv supplied, this little after, thought of his seems a little behind time.

As to your editorial comment that " the Corporation Commissioners were anxious to be rid of the Inquiry, at the earliest possible moment," if you think 11 months of patient consideration "the earliest possible moment" we must leivo the public to jut-v. It may interest the public to know that the printed results of our labours numbers some 440 large folio pages—now at the disposal of the Mayor and Corporation.— Ytturs _ _

T. BIRCHA.LL.

P.S.—As Mr. Williatns wrote me from the House of Commons giving no other address, I c3uld not then reply to him personally which as a rnltter of friendly courtesy I should wish t 3 have d me.

WARRINGTON ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE.

Yesterday's Appeals. A meeting of the Assessment Committee was held at the Workhouse yesterday. Mr. A. J. Jolley presided, and there were also present :—Messrs. A. J. Jolley, Billinge, Hickman, Davies, Mather, Brooks, Houghton, Forshaw, Ashcroft, and J. C. Sutton (clerk).

HA.YDOCK PARK RACECOURSE.

Mr. Kirkham, solicitor, against the assessment of the Ha,ydock Park racecourse, the present assessment of which is gross £2,500, rateable £2,250. Mr. Kirkham pointed out that the racecourse company had been advised by counsel of the highest eminence that the Assessment Committee ought in assessing the course, to take into considera- tion what rent it would let to a yearly tenant. Racing generally was a very risky undertaking. If they missed a meeting on account of the weather it was lost and was never replaced. Racing was entirely under the control of the Jockey Club, and they could cut off, if they chose, one, two, or three meetings. Although they had not yet held the whole of the meetings,the five years just elapsed had been exceptionally lucky. He had nothing to conceal from the committee, they would turn their books inside out. The Haydock Board were an exceptionally competent Board, and he wished it to be distinctly understood that they had nothing to do with the Manchester Race Company. It was quite distinct although there were a few of the same share- holders in each company. Mr. Kirkham contended that the assessment should be seduced to £1,486. The Clerk said it would be impossible to decide the matter that day. It would have to go to a reference. In answer to Mr. Billinge, Mr. Kirkham said the aver- age number of meetings a year was seven. It was decided to refer the matter for further c9n- sideration to the Clerk, valuer the and the assistant overseer of Haydock. "THE OLD HOUSE AT HOME."

Mr. Hatton (on behalf of Messrs. Robert Davies and Company) appealed against the assessment of the "Old House at Home," 4, Barbauld-street, Friars-green now assessed at £BO gross, and £6B rateable. Mr. Hatton said the house belonged to Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Company, Prior to the alterations it was let at a rental of £35 and it was assessed at £45 gross. The alterations which had bean made recently were under- taken onaccount of the objection of thelicensing bench to any living rooms being used for trade purposes. Formerly, the kitchen was used for trade purposes, but since the alterations, the room formerly the kitchen had been used solely for business purposes. Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Company had increased the rent by £l5 making it £5O. The assess- ment had been raises from £45 to £BO. It was that increase he complained of. His point was that for trading purposes, the house, since the alterations, was not one whit better than it was before. The whole of the additions were for private purposes. The altera- tions were completed on the Ist of May and the sales for the three months since that date were slightly less than for the corresponding months last year. He sug- gested that a fair assessment would be £l2 gross. He admitted that an old beerhouse in the immediate vicin- ity called the "Star and Garter" had been given up. It was a wretched place and its closing had made no difference to the trade of the "Old House at Home."— The committee decided to reduce the assessment from £BO gross, £6B rateable to 275 gross, £65 rateable.

Additions are being erected, but they are not yet roo fed in. .No further are% of the works is as yet in use. Additional machinery is being placed in the fine wire room and in the perforating shop; but it is not used at present as not being completed. • Messrs. cartons, Limited, appealed against their assessment of warehouse in Friars-green, gross £95, rateable £76. Inasmuch as the office building is at present out of use, lam of Opinion the assessment might be reduced to gross .87105., rateable £70." The report was adopted.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL

The Pollution of Sankey Broak. LONGFORD DEPOT NUISANCE.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Berney Chambers on Wednes- day afternoon, Mr. T. Halton presiding. There were also present :—Dr. Murray, Messrs. CI Mather, A. Brooks, (vice-chairman), J. W. Knowles, EL Forshaw, W. Houghton, with Dr. Sephton (medical officer of health). Messrs. J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. Kerfoot (inspector of nuisances , B. S. New- comb (surveyor), and IL H. &Image (assistant sur- veyor).

===== 0002601_19040806_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
CUERDLEY WATER SUPPLY.

The Council• has recently had this question under con- sideration, and the Clerk now made a short statement. He pointed out that the matter would probably involve a dispute between Widnes and Liverpool as the right of water supply. He would not advice the Council to enter upon that part of the ques- tion. Widnes and Liverpool should settle the point between themselves. All the Council had to do was to see that there was a water supply to Cuerdley in some shape or form. There might be an effort to have Cuerdley included in Prescot Union, but when that arose he would call a meeting of the committee appointed to deal with the water question.

BURTONWOOD AND CORPORATION WATER. • DIFFICULTY AS TO PRICE.

There was considerable discussion with reference to the supply of water to Bartonwood by the Warrington Corporation. The Clerk pointed out that prior to the last meeting, a letter was received from the water engineer stating that in future all extensions in th township must be guaranteed, at the same percentage as was charged] to all the outlying townships. Thate percentage at the present time was 8 per cent on the cost of the work for a term of seven years. Mr. Sutton explained that originally the Corporation entered into an arrangement for the supply of water to Bartonwood at 5 per cent guarantee. There was no doubt they afterwards found they could not do it at that rate. He (Mr. Sutton) had maintained that the Corporation were bound under the first agreement, but there was a clause stating " to supply such places in Burtonwood with water as the Corporation of Warrington and this Coun- cil may from time to time agree." The Corporation contended that they were not bound to supply any places, but that they must be' parties to the arrangement. That meant that they could decline to agree. At the last meeting the Council decided that water should be supplied to several places on the terms specified by the Corporation, and an agreement was now submitted in which the Corporation undertook to supply all such places as should be agreed upon from time to time. He understood there was a fair prospect that in a time not very distant the receipts from a number of places to which the mains were being extended would almost equal the 8 per cent. Mr. FORSFIA.W said he could not agree to anything except 5 per cent. He objected to the original agree- ment being dispensed with. The CLERK said that if the Council did not come to an agreement the Corporation would refuse to supply any further water in Burtonwood, except to the places mentioned in the old agreement. He had received a letter from Mr. Fairclough, clerk to the Parish Council, stating that at a parish meeting a resolution was unani- mously passed protesting against the action of the District Council in sanctioning the increased percentage and thereby increasing the responsibility of the rate- payers of the parish. Mr. Fortsna.w said lie would not agree to any pay- ment otherwise than in conformity with the original agreement. If the increased charge was persisted in, he would call a public meeting and have the matter sub- mitted to the Local Government Board. The Corpora- tion had made an agreement and they ought to conform to it.

The CLERK said the Corporation carried out:the agree- ment until they found they were losing money by it. Mr. FORSHAW moved that the new agreement be not sanctioned.

Mr. KNOWLES said he 'understood a parish meeting passed a resolution that everyone in Bartonwood should be supplied with water' and that was the reason why the Council had agreed to the new terms. The CLERK said the Town Clerk promised him that if he could !Ind that it would pay the Corporation to con dune the supply at 5 per cent. or less than 8 per cent., he would not object to the old arrangement remaining in force. Having gone into the figures, Mr. Whittle found they could not supply the water at the old price. He (Mr. Sutton) would have Mr. Forshaw bear in mind that the more customers they obtained the less likely the parish was to have to pay the peraentage. Eventually it was agreed thit the matter should lie in abeyance fora month. In the meantime Mr. Forshaw can make further inquiries,and if necessary call a parish meeting.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====

Mr. HOUGHTON raised the question of the water supply in the district generally. He had, he said, heard that morning that not a drop of water could be obtained at the smallpox hospital at Sankey. The CHAIRMAN said that morning it took fully a quarter of an hour to get a bucket of water at his house.

Dr. MURRAY said that during half a day they had been unable to obtain a drop of water at Penketh Friends' School.

The CIiA.TRNI tsked what use it would be to pay for water for fire extinguishing purposes if hey could not get a bucketful of water from the tans in the houses. That had been the case for three or four months in the Sankey district. The Inspector was instructed to make inquiries as to the water supply in the Sankey neighbourhood.

WAGES INCREASED.

On the recommendation of the Parish Council, it was decided to increase the wages of Thomas Whit- field, night soil contractor, hy £7 105. per annum.

THE SUGGESTED CONTRICUT. lON TO FIRE BRIGADE EXPENSES. _ ___

The Clerk read' a letter from the Town Clerk asking to be furnished with the rateable value of such of the townships as the Council thought should have the services of the Warrington Fire Brigade. The establish- ment expenses averaged £525 per annum for the last five years and he thought that each authority having the services of the brigade should contribute to such expense in proportion to the rateable value. The town- ship should as far as possible be kept within a five mile radius of the Borough, but if a part only came within the 5 miletlimit the Corporation were prepared to allow the Brigade to attend fires in the whole of such town- ship. The CLERK pointed out that in the Warrington Rural District theve were two rateable values on the buildings and land respectively. Nearly the whole of Warrington consisted of houses with great liability to catch fire, whereas the country parishes included many fields. It seemed to him rhat the charge should be made accord- ing to the rateable value of the property that was most liable to take fire. Mr. FORSHA.W said the Parish Council of Burtonwood had been in commuuication with the Town Clerk and had agreed to pay £1 or 30s. per annum. Mr. KNOWLES : That will be another mistake, I think on the part of Burtonwood. It was agreed that the Clerk should endeavour to arrange satisfactory terms, and report at the next meeting.

RIGHT OF WAY QUESTION AT BURTONWOOD. THE CLERK SUGGESTS A SIMPLE REMEDY.

The CLERK announced that the nest matter concerned Burtonwood. Mr. BROOKS : Is this the Burtonwood District Council ? (Laughter.) Mr. FORSHAW : Burtonwood is the largest township in the district. It ought to be an urban authority. Mr. KNOWLES : it would be a pleasure to be rid of it. (Laughter.) The CLERK read a letter from the Clerk to the Burton- wood Parish Council stating that the footpath at the south end of Mercer-street, leading from Clay-lane, across Ashton farm, and adjoining the footpath from the Church to the old smithy in Cow-lane, had been closed to the public, an iron fence having keen placed across. The owners (Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons, Warrington) refused to remove the obstruction. The District Council was requested to take proceedings in the matter.

• The CLERK said he had always held that if an old footpath was o •structed, the people who had the right ! to use it could go and take away that obstruction. That was a very simple remedy. The Council was invited to enter into litigation with Messrs. Fairclough as to , whether it was a public footpath or not. No action was taken in the matter. BUTTERMILK BRIDGE. I Mr.Jositrit Smmt,clerk to the Sankey Parish Council, wrote stating that at the last meeting of that authority attention was drawn to the condition of Buttermilk Bridge, and he was instructed to write the District Council stating that the Parish Council were pleased to hear that it was proposed to approach the Corporation i with the idea of having an iron bridge. The present wooden bridge was considered dangerous, and the Council hoped to hear that a new and•more suitable bridge had been decided upon at an early date. The SURVEYOR said he approached the Corporation through Mr. Longdin, some time ago, but they refused to take any part in the expense of a new bridge. The present bridge had been repaired and was quite safe.

THE POLLUTION OF SA-NK EY BROOK: WHO ARE TILE CHIEF onimsbEns ?

Mr. Joseph Smith also wrote that at the meeting of Sankey Parish Council it was reported that complaints were constantly being made relative to the nuisance

created by Sankey Brook. The Council had appointed two members—Messrs. Furness and Jefferson—to wait upon the District Council in the hope that an interview might be of some assistance in bringing about an abate- ment of the nuisance.

Messrs. Furness and Jefferson attended the meeting and Mr. FURNESS spoke on the matter. He said he did not think he need make any apology for coming before the Council. The complaints about the condition of Sankey Brook were a "hardy annual," but the Parish Council had recently received such numerous complaints that they felt I bound to take special notice of them. Their object in approaching the District Council was to see if something could not be done with the Local Government Board if not to abolish, at least to minimise the nuisance. Mr. Jefferson and he had taken the trouble to follow the brook as far as the asylum, and they found that the asylum was not polluting the brook, as the effluent was of a very satisfactory character. Practically the whole of the sewage of the north end of Warrington was turned into the brook a Ilittle lower down, and the gas works also discharged a very objectionable effluent. At Longford depot 120 or 1130 thousand gallons of water were taken from the brook for condensing purposes, but were after- ward turned into the stream warm, making it still more offensive. He had interviewed the Town Clerk who said that if he (Mr. Furness) could in- duce the District Council to bring pressure to bear on the Local Government Board with the object of mini- mising the nuisance above the Warrington area, the Corporation would give an undertaking at once to put their section in good order. On referring to old news- papers he found that the state of Sankey Brook had come up for discussion almost annually for the past thirty-six years. Thirty-eight years ago a Cornmission was held at which it was stated that no brook of the character of Sankey Brook would be allowed to con- tinue in that condition in any other part of England The CHAIRNIAN said he understood a few years ago that St. Helens were going to treat theircbemical refuse before turning it into the brook. Mr. FURNESS said he believed St. Helens were com- pelled to treat their sewage matter. A special clause was inserted in the last Act of Parliament that no objectionable effluent must be turned into the brook. Mr. KNOWLES remarked that if the point where pol- luting matter was turned into the brook was within the Council's district, he did not see why they could not proceed for an injunction. The CLERK said one half of the brook was in the borough and the other half was in Great Sankey. The District Council were not altogether clean in respect, of it. They had some little—but it was only a little— contribution to the pollution, but he thought the report Mr. Newcombe had presented went a long way in the direction of suggested means of treating their sewage" without turning it into the brook. The greater part of the nuisance was from the borough; there was no doubt about that. That Council, however, contributed and Mr. Newcombe ought to devise means of treating the sewage without its getting into the brook. That was all they could do as a Council and they would have done their duty when they did that. The SURVEYOR said the sewage from their district *as such a small matter that they could treat it for next to nothing. The CHAIRMAN said he had complained of the nuisance time after time for years, but the Council had always had the drawback of knowing that they tapped the brook with their own sewage. Mr. KNowLEs said that at Causeway Bridge, Burton- wood, there was hardly any smell under ordinary condi- tions. The brook was always bad between there and Sankey. The SURVEYOR said St. Helens Corporation had recently opened a new sewer, and he was afraid sewage would be turned into the brook as long as they (the members of the District Council) lived, at any rate. Dr. SEPIITON said the sewage of St. Helens was dis- charged into the brook without being purified in any way. The CLERK said be wisthed to call attention to the desirableness, as far as the District Council was concerned, of treating their sewage. The Surveyor said he could manage to treat it in each parish at a comparatively small cost. In that case they would be freed of any charge that they polluted the brook. That week he had received the report of the County Medical Officer of Health. In the returns,the Warrington Rural District joined four others. The average death rate for the Wigan District was 16.8 ; Prescot, 21.7 • Barton- upon-Irwell, 12.8; Warrington, 12.2. On ihe other hand, the borough of Warrington had ong of the heaviest death rates in the country. It was to the credit of that Council's district that they had such a low rate. If the figures included the deaths at Winwick Asylum—and he saw no evidence to the contrary—the rate for the dis- trict proper was still lower. It was not for Warrington to call upon that Council to have the brook cleared above their district. Their duty Was to take means to treat their own sewage. The CHAIRMAN said the brook must be detrimental to health. The Sankey Parish Council ought to go more deeply into the matter, to see if a scheme of deal- ing with the Sankey sewage could not be arranged. , On the proposition of Dr. MURRAY, seconded by Mr. !KNOWLES, the Surveyor was instructed to prepare a report with reference to the sewage of Great Sankey land submit it at the next meeting. HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. TWO DEATHS FROM SMALLPDX. Dr. SEPHTON reported that during the month there had been 25 births and 36 deaths. The death rate was 379 per 1,000 against 30.8 the average for the past three months, and 35.7 for the corresponding month last, year. Two deaths from smallpox had occurred in Sankey sanatorium. The patients came from Bank-st. Warrington. Sixteen deaths were reported from the . - County Asylum and exclusive of these, and the deaths at the sanatorium the death-rate proper for the district was 17'8.

BURTONWOOD BROOK POLLUTION. Dr. SEPHTON reported that he met the Surveyor and inspector at Burtonwood, and examined the brook from Mr. Forshaw's brewery to the sewerage works. Very little water was running down the brook which was in bad condition and very offensive. The brewery tanks were fairly clean. The effluent was so treated that at stagnant points sewage was precipitated into the brook.. The officials gave such directions as they thought neces- sary to improve the foal condition of the brook. A fish about two inches long was seen swimming in the effluent. How it got there they did not know. (Laugh- ter.) The report was adopted. LONGFORD DEPOT. The Town Clerk wrote with reference, to the notice served upon the Corporation to abate the nuisance at Longford Depot. Great difficulties had been experi— enced during the last few weaks owing to the scarcity of water, but the Corporation were now constructing a reservoir in order to enable the committee to obtain an ample supply, and they were carrying out other works at great expense with the object of minimising as far as possible any cause of complaint. The Chairman of the committee and himself would be glad to meet the inspector on the spot to discuss the matter. The Inspector and Medical Officer were deputed to interview the Chairman and Town Clerk as suggested. THE BROOK AT ORFORD. Messrs. Mortimer and Co., Orford Tannery,wrote with reference to the notice served upon them to abate a nuisance alleged to be caused by the discharge of refuse into the brookat Orford. At the present monnint,at con- siderable expense,they were having"this so-called brook" thoroughly cleansed. They were at all times anxious to do their utmost, and they felt sure the Council would not wish to put insurmountable difficulties in the way of carrying on a business which employed a con- siderable amount of labour---in fact, gave employment to the whole village of Orford. They employed con- stantly one man who did nothing but take solid matter, as far as possible, out of the brook. They did not think, as far as the objectionable smell was concerned, that they were.the chief offenders. The brook was a common sewer for all the effluents of the parish. The CHAIRMAN said it seemed that the firm were trying to do their best to abate the nuisance. The Inspector and Medical Officer were instructed to visit the works.

The CHAIRMAN having declared the meeting closed, Mr. Forshaw rose to introduce other matters, and a warm passage of arms ensued between the representa- tive of Burtonwood and Mr. Knowles. The latter declared that Mr. Forsha,w had been elected by a "mob of cottagers."

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

BOY'S INGENIOUS THEFTS. A small boy named James Morris, of Owen-street,was charged at the Warrington Police Court on Monday with stealing from the person of William Griffiths,aged eight years,3s.6d. the money of Joseph Griffiths,and also with stealing is. from the person of Arthur Potter whilst in the Winwick-street Co-operative Stores on Friday.— Superintendent Monaghan said the boy Griffiths was sent on an errand to the Co-operative Stores, carrying the 3s. 6d. which was wrapped in paper. He met the prisoner who, under pretence of telling his fortune, asked him to look through a shop window. Griffiths did so, and prisoner snatched the money from his hand and made off. On Friday the boy Potter was in the stores when the prisoner came to him and said if he would give him his money he would get the goods more quickly than he (Potter) could. Potter gave prisoner the shilling, and he bolted. He was arrested by Detective Meacock.— Prisoner was ordered to receive eight strokes with the birch and to be kept in custody until six o'clock.

TWO THINGS TO AVOID.

IRREGULAR MEALS AND HEAVY SMOKING.

MERSEY PIER-MASTER'S VIEWS.

"It's my usual time, but I can't stop to eat just now. I I'll have a pipe instead." Thus men are always finding excuses for taking their meals at irregular hours. Further they are constantly injuring their digestion by heavy smoking. Both these habits should be carefully avoided or disastrous consequences may ensue. Making some inquiries upon the subject, a reporter was favoured with the views of some well-known Runcorn men. One of them, Mr. John Carlsen, held that smoking was a luxury for which he had himself paid dearly. "I have good cause to think so," he said, "for I attribute an illness I had to continual smoking more than to anything else, though irregular meals had something to do with it. Did I get better when I stopped smoking? Well, it, took more than that to cure me. I was downright ill but thanks to Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, I am hale and hearty again. It began in this way," continued Mr. Carlsen, who is pier-master at Weston, near Runcorn," I am exposed to all kinds of weather, and I took my meals at irregular times. Frequently I fast- ed for many hours and returned home (I live at Quarry Bank-terrace, Weston, by the way), wet through. My , constitution at length weakened. I felt 'seedy 'and , tired. Although my appetite was not affected very much, ' my digestive organs seemed to be altogether ought of order. When I came home from work, I had always to lie down and rest. "Upon the suggestion of a friend, I tried Dr. Williams' pink pills, and bafore I had taken three boxes, I felt much better. I continued with the medicine, and soon I was quite well again. Being a heavy smoker, I thought my pipe was at the bottom of the mischief. You cannot be always smoking and not suffer somehow. "But Dr.Williams' pink pills not only cured me then, but quite recently when T had a bad attack of sciatica the pills gave me immediate relief." Mrs. Carlsen, who Joined in the conversation also gladly testified to the value of the medicine. " Some years ago," she said "I met with a cycling accident. My right arm was much hurt. • "For ever so long after, it remained troublesome, and a touch of rheumatism made it very painful. Eventually I tried Dr. Williams' pink pills, and I benefited very much." Mrs. Carlson added that, having derived much benefit, she had had no hesitation in recommending the pills to her friends. Two young ladies who came to live in the district suffered from antemia, and upon Mrs. Carlsen's advice they took a few boxes of Dr. Williams' pink pills, and before long were practically cured. "We alsoknow,", said Mrs. Carlsen a man who was inclined to be consumptive, but after a fair trial of Dr.Williams' pink pills for pale people, he speaks in the highest praise of them.'

===== 0002601_19040806_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SMOKE NUISANCE AT

WARRINGTON.

A Remarkable Offer.

The latest triumph of the Smoke Dragon says "The Dawn" is to drive away from Warrington the Clergy Daughters' School. Not content with darkening our skies, and dirtying our windows, and murdering our trees and flowers,it has continually breathed its baleful breath right in the eyes of this old institution, and, after nestling on Mote Hill for sixty years, it last month left us, in despair, to seek a haven amid the green and smil- ing hills of Derbyshire. We would sooner have lost a dozen chimneys! But nobody appears to care,and we were told quite recently by a good friend who means us well, that we had better not say anything against smoke, as the workingman would not like it. Poor old working- man! How they do bamboozle and delude him! 1f he only knew, he is the greatest sufferer, for he has to live, and breathe, and die amid the blackness. We have always said that so much smoke was so much waste; that it was possible not only to remove the nuisance, but effect a consequential saving; that, instead of check- ing true industrial enterprize, our plea, if carried out, would greatly further it. It, seems, after all, that we were right. The chairman of the Electricity Com- mittee—a committee which, for months, was num- bered with the chief transgressors—tells us, now, with tardy wisdom, that he and his illustrious confreres have at last diqcovered that it if: possible to save well on for fifty per cent. in fuel and prevent black smoke entirely by the use of mechanical stokers. Since then, we have been privileged to see 'Messrs. Crosfield's Bank Quay Works. And the chimneys really are practically smoke- less. And the firm's expert—perhaps the ablest chemist in the town—was good enough to tell us that his com- pany were saving thousands by their. present system, and that other local manufactures might do the same ; that even the ironworks—the chief offenders—though with greater difficulty, might achieve the same result; and that he was prepared to instal in any works in Warrington, entirely at his own fixpense, a plant which would accomplish all he claimeda he were guaranteed a fair percentage of the profit which, he said, would follow from the consequent ecOnomy in coal. He added that he was willing to give one hundred pounds to the Infirmary if he should fail, and that we were at liberty to publicly repeat what he had said.
