RETALI AT il_P .

MR. BALFOUR'S PANACEA.

TAX THOSE WHO TAX US.

THE position of the Government in relation to the Fiscal question is quite unique. A Fortion of its members are nominal Free traders, a larger number are Protectionists, Ind the rest are Retaliators. The policy of t-he Government up to the election is Free Trade:. after then it is to be Retaliation, with an easy option to any of its members to follow Mr. Chamberlain if they choose. The Prime Minister believes that our present Fiscal policy ought to be reversed, but he finds it inexpedient to reverse it.

The Government's Powers,

MR. BALFOUR always speaks as though some special Act of Parliament would be necessary in order to carry out his policy of Retaliation. lie tells us we must recover our power of •oegotiation with foreign countries. As a matter of fact, no new powers are required, and no Act of Parliament is needed. All that is necessary to put Retaliation in force is for the Foreign Office and the Treasury to work togethef--the one to inform foreign Govern- ments that unless they reduce taxes upon British goods we shall place taxes on their goods; the other to make such arrangements - -rrould b necessary to impose the taxes.

as wouiL Jo sary_ Finally, such taxes would only have to ,be included in the next Budget and the thing is done. Why, if Mr. Balfour believes that Retaliation is necessary, he does not carry out these arrangements it is difficult to say.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Retaliation and Free Foed.

MR. BALFOUR is supposed to be opposed to any taxes Upon food or raw material. Whether such opposition arises from belief in the wisdom- of free imports of food, or whether it is due to his fear of tile electors, is not quite clear. From his speeches we are led to believe that he opposes the taxation of food simply because such taxes would be unpopular. He contends that his policy of Retaliation would not involve the taxation of food. On the other hand, Free Traders argue that it would be impossible for Great Britain to retaliate.xpon_food,producing countries unless the Government had power to put a tax upon corn. Unfortunately, in this contest with Mr. Lalfour we have nothing clear or definite to o upon. He tells us P-he is in favour of Retaliation, but nothing more. He has never condescended to enter- into details or explain his 'schemes. Unlike Mr. Chamberlain, he has not considered himself to be a missionary of a new Fiscal gospel. His only mission in life seems to be to invent excuses for keeping the çi esent Government in power. He thus places his opponents in a difficulty by refusing to st ate the . issue upon which they are to meet him.

Retaliation may mean anything or nothing. Its chief advantage is that it will allow Mr. Balfour to develop consistently into a Protec- tionist, or equally consistently remain a Free Trader. What the people of this country think of this shifty policy is best seen by the, results of the recent elections. They have rejected Mr. Balfour's Retaliation with as n licb freedom as they have refused to accPnt Mr. Chamberlain's Protection. In fact, the country has declined to distinguish between the two. In this respect the electors have drawn public notice to a simple economic truth. You cannot put Retaliation into force without taxing food, and the constituencies have preferred not to run any risks by return- ing Retaliation candidates.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
Food-Producing Countries.

A GLANCE at the figures and origin of our imported food will shew how foolish it is to think we could retaliate upon foreign countries and still import free food. The two countries which impose the heaviest duties upon British goods are the United States of America and Russia. Any scheme of Retaliation would necessarily involve the taxation of the imports received from those countries. Now what do those countries send us ? Here are the figures for 1002:

===== 0002601_19040806_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
From Russia.

Food and Raw Materials £22,589,000 Manufactures

Total From United States.

Food and Raw Materials £106,031,000 Manufactures

• Total £126,962,000 From the above figures we see that any Retaliation upon the manufactures sent us by Russia and the United States would not be likely to influence the Governments or people of those countries. The only way to make them " feel " our retaliation would be to tax the food they send us. Unfortunately, we should "feel" that ourselves far more than they would. The Hitter Hit.

MR. CHAMBERLAIN has informed the world that he does not like being hit without hitting back. That is a very dangerous principle to apply to the trade of this country; but it is one which is very popular with a certain class of people. In the Oswestry division the walls were covered with Tory bills bearing the following inscription: Treat other nations as they treat us. Tax

those who tax you. There we have the whole gospel of Retaliation. i But, as we have said, it s a very dangerous gospel. Fortunately or unfortunately, the trade of Great Britain is so enormous that we present a larger surface to be hit than any of our competitors. Thus the exports of manufactured goods from the chief countries are as follows:

Exports of Manufactures, 1901. United States .. £85,000,000 France .. 90,000,000

Germany • • . . 144, 000, 000 Great Britain .. .. 229,000,000 When we remember that the total export of British goods to the sqlf-governing Colonies is only some £60,000,000, we shall realise how great is our trade with foreign countries, and to what a lamp extent our prosperity depends upon maintaining friendly trade relations with them. It is all very well to talk about hitting back, but if we start hitting our own customers the result will be disastrous. For every blow we gave them they could give us two.

• Holy Trinity Church Renovation. lAt a recent meeting of the Holy Trinity Church Coun- cil, the subject of church renovation came under con- sideration along with (funds permitting) the substitu- tion of electric light for gas in the whole church. It had been thought feasible to do the work during the next few weeks, but, after consideration, it was decided to postpone it until the spring, which will enable the lwardens, with the assistance of the congregation, to raise the balance of the money required. £BO is already set aside for renovation purposes, but a considerable additional sum will be needed.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
GLORIOUS WEATHER AND A SUCCESSFUL MEETING.

Ideal holiday weather and the prop' .t Of an excel- lent afternoon's sport attracted a crowd of between four and five thousand to the Wilderspool ground on Monday, when the Warrington Football Club held their nineteenth annual athletic festival. Without these sports, Warrington's August Bank Holiday would be regarded as incomplete, and the meeting loses none of its popularity as years go on. Monday's condi- tions could hardly have been more favourable, and from two o'clock the turnstiles clicked merrily. The programme was long, but the arrangements were ad- mirable in every respect, and the events were got through in quick time. Some of the foot races were characterised by poor turnouts of entrants, and the result was that several competitors had easy tasks in winning their heats. The substitution of a two miles walking handicap for the boxing contest was an entirely new feature, and the spectators derived no little amusement from the many styles of pedestrianism. Interest seemed to flag somewhat before the long distance was covered. As usual the cycle races furnished plenty of excitement, and it was a source of satisfaction to the assembly that two the well- known local riders, carried off prizes. There were one or two spills but, fortunately, the wheelers escaped uninjured. In most of the handicaps the interest was maintained throughout, and the scholars' contest again proved a success. Selections by the Warrington Borough brass band and Miss Turner's mandoline band were credit- ably given, and served to enliven the proceedings. To the club officials special praise is due for the time and care which they had devoted to the arrangements. The various duties were undertaken, as follows : Clerk of the course, Mr. T. Emmanuel Church, Bold-street.

Two important events took place on Monday in con- nection with the above church. The first was the arrival of a consignment of the new edition of the Revised Book of Common Prayer. The other event was that a party of the congregation departed on an after- noon trip. The weather was all that heart could wish. when upwards of 80 members and friends of the church set off to Delamere Forest. Two large vehicles afforded accommodation and the day will long be remembered among the happy events in the history of Emmanuel Church. Bowling along the well-kept roads, the native charms of Cheshire scenery were much enjoyed. On arrival the party sat down to a substantial meal to which ample justice was done. Thus fortified,pleasant rambles were indulged in,and the forest glens soon resounded with the peals of merry laughter of the holiday-makers. The journey home was commenced about 7 p.m., and proved a delightful cool sequel to the heat of the run and the games in the woods. The rabbits and hares came out for inspection, and their movements were eagerly watched. Volunteers waved their greetings from their Icamping ground as the party passed. Arpley Station was reached about 10.30 p.m., where a hymn and the Benediction brought a delightful day to a close.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
Consecration of Newchurch Church.

The Lord Bishop of Liverpool consecrated the new and handsom 3 church which has been erected at New- church on S tturdav, the special service being largely attended. In the course of his sermon his lordship said there were two sidls of conse3ration—t,he religious side and the legal side. That day, with prayer and praise, they had consecrated that church, that was to say, set it apart from all ordinary and common uses, and dedicated it for ever to the worship of Almighty God. It would wound their feelings, it would scandalize their minds if in the future that beautiful house of prayer was used for a social or political gathering. They had listened to the words of prayer and praise which had gone up to GO, and the words of consecration. Hence- forth, the building was for ever a holy one, and as they stepped across its threshold, they bowed their heads, they talked in whispers, they moved about slowly and reverently, for it was a house of God. But conse- cration had also a legal aspect. A deed of consecration had been signed and witnessed, and it would be preserved among the documents of the dioceie.and n iu.; ;i of an Act of Parliament could cause the church to be pulled down or put to any purpose other than that for the wor- ship of Almighty God.ln concluding,his lordship said the Church crept and crawled where once she advanced by leaps and bounds, because of the divi4ions of Christen- dom and because of the bitterness of Christian people one to another. They were, however, bound to contend for the faith once delivered to the saints, and they must not forget that whilst they were truly Catholic they were equally truly Protestant. The true Protestant was not a man who dealt with negations, but one who held fast to the Catholic faith. At the close of the consecration service a confirmation service was conducted by the Lord Bishop.

Ebenezer Church, Butterm3rket-street. The New Pastor.

On Thursday evening the tmarnbers and friends of the above church, to the number of 100, assembled for an excellent tea kindly provided by Councillor Thornton, .P. The object of the gathering was to welcome the ew Pastor, the Rev. Alfred Barrett, formerly of Hatton. After tea a short service was held. Councillor Thornton occupied the chair, and in his opening remarks referred to the past history of the church, and expressed the best wishes for its future prosperity.—The next speaker was Mr. Langton, lay evangelist from St. Peter's Church. He addressed some very encouraging remarks to all present, exhorting every one to do his or her utmost to make the Church as great a successes possible. Mr. Roberts, a well-known prize tenor, then rendered The Holy City" excellently, and was heartily applauded. Mr. Merlin addressed the meeting, and he also encouraged the people and asked them all to unite and work together. The Chairman introduced the new Pastor with a few witty remarks and a proposition that the heartiest reception possible he accorded him.—The proposition was seconded by Mr. Merlin, and after a hearty clapping of hands the Pastor commenced his address. His opening query was "How shall we make my ministry a success ?" He said there could be no real success without working hard for it. If a man did not work in these days, and work hard, he was soon pushed aside and fell into the rear. The first quality which made for success was industry,the second, having a direct aim. Without industry and a direct aim there could be no real success, as the world had numberless purposeless geople in it. The Pastor's address, which was very impressive and inspiring, included several amusing anecdotes. At the close, the reverend gentle- man received very hearty and appreciative applause. Mr. Roberts then sang by special request the Welsh solo "Y Cartref Dedwydd Fry " (" The Heavenly Rest ") which received the applause it so well deserved. Light refreshments were then handed round, and the Doxology was sung, thus bringing a most enjoyable meeting to a close. •

FEARNHEAD SEWAGE.

Rural Council Surveyor's Suggestions For Its Disposal.

In view of the agitation to improve the condition of Sankey Brook, it is interesting to note that Mr. B. S. Newcombe, surveyor to the Rural District Council, has prepared a report containing suggestions for the treat- ment of the sewage of Poulton-with-Fearnhead. It reads as follows:—In accordance with instruction given at your June meeting, Dr- Sephton, Mr. Kerfoot and myself have met on the ground and examined into the feasibility of treating the sewage locally. _

BrAcKi3Roox.—At- Blackbro-ok there is very little fall, quite insufficient to treat bacteriologically, and the only method practicable will be to construct a tank close to the present outlet and to precipitate the sewage with alumino-ferric. This principle (although not so satisfactory as the septic system), would remove the nuisance at present existing. Probable cost, approximate £.lO. ORCHARD-STREET.—Fortunately the levels taken of the Orchard-street area shew that by re-laying the lines from the various cesspools (to save fall at present wasted), and piping two ditches there will remain a fall of 27 inches wherewith to treat the sewage sceptically. At the same time I would suggest that the Farmer's Arms Hotel and the block of property opposite, be also connected to the same system it being quite feasible. Both ditches would be required to be piped and sewage collected and cdrried, say 50 yards below the corner of old Orchard. Probable cost, approximate £l5O. PADGAT E-L ANE.--Unfortunately, at the troublesome point here, there is absolutely no spare fall, and the land is quite waterlogged and stagnant. The only system to be recommended as feasible (that is without pumping or putting the Township to permanent expense) would be as follows :—To carry the present pipe across the brook with an oval of flat box pipe, obstructing the brook as little as possible. To lay a new main alongside the brook at foot of Mr. James Houghton's garden to , divert the brook straight across second field to save fall and to treat the sewage matter there—similarly to that 'it Blackbrook ; namely, with trough and alumino-ferric basket and to precipitate in a settling tank. At the same time perhaps I should point out that the land here is so low-lying as to be often entirely under water during the winter months. But with the fall at hand I am of the opinion that it would be quite impossible to treat it in any better manner. Probable cost,approxi- mate, £75. In Conclusion I might say that although the suggested treatment by precipitation with alumino-ferric would not be a perfect system or as thorough as the septic and filtration system, it would at any rate remove all solids and flocculent matters frou the sewage and deodorize it. The effluent would be very fair and quite harmless to the public health. The cost of attention, cleansing and supply of alumino-ferric would of course be an annual charge upon the Township; but thia would be minute as compared with any of the larger systems recently suggested.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR NOTES.

Inspired by the excellent example set by the Motor , Cycling Club, we note that several provincial clubs are now instituting non-stop runs of lengthy duration. These are proving extremely interesting, whilst they certainly provide the most satisfactory means of prov- ing the reliability—or otherwise—of the different machines.

mine Although a two-speed gear is not really necessary upon the ordinary, pedal-propelled bicycle, it is certainly a boon upon the motor cycle, and especially in those , cases where a free engine is fitted. Not only is every ascent thus rendered possible without pedalling, but a higher speed is attainable on the level, since the second speed can be geared higher than is advisable where no such device is employed.

Where water-cooling is employed upon motor cycles (and this is greatly resorted to now that higher-powered engines and fore-carriages have become popular), the Thermo-syphon system is preferable to circulation by mearns of a mechanically operated pump. Provided that the water tank is placed at a point above the level of the top of the engine, success is assured, since the very laws of natural science demand that the water shall circulate properly. A pump adds considerably to the complications of the water-cooling arrangements, and is really not imperative save for the engine of a high or moderately high h.p. car.

The Thermo-syphon system is employed upon many small cars and voiturettes, and with these it proves per- fectly satisfactory, conditionally, of course, upwn the arrangement just mentioned. Obviously, therefore, it is quite suitable for a motor cycle. it may be added that the pipes and glands should be of large diameter in order that any possible cause of trouble shall be eliminated.

It is a little surprising, and sometimes extremely annoying as Well, to find how little provision has been made for the proper reception of tools, spare parts, inner tubes, &c., upon cars that in other respects are well equipped. A small locker under each of the tonneau seats is a great convenience. Spare tubes with the repairing outfit can be neatly tucked away in one of them out of sight, and, moreover, out of the proximity of a possibly leaky oilcan. Necessary spares can be carried in the toolbox which is usually situated under one of the front seats, so that the other locker can be utilised for oddments of luggage.

As it is not always possible to cram all the necessary tools into the somewhat minute space customarily pro- vided for the purpose, and it is uncomfortable for the passengers in the tonneau to be routed from their seats whilst a hunt for a screw-wrench of particular dimen- sions is in progress, it is a wonder more of the makers do not follow the device adopted on the new Decauville cars and utilize the small space between the arms of the two bucket seats in front, by forming it into a neat if irreplarly-shapd box. In this can be put the cotton waste, pliers, adjustable spanner, with any other small tool likely to be needed in the case of a roadside adjust- ment becoming necessary. A flap lid is fitted and the receptacle so devised and so very accessible and handy that it is a wonder some such plan is not oftener used.

There have been some bad cases of furious and reck- less driving lately reported—and not only reported, but proved beyond a doubt. Drivers cannot be too careful, even though they are assured that they have their cars under complete control, and anything like "cutting it fine" in over-taking or passing other vehicles on the road is to be condemned. The best driver living has no guarantee that his car will not side-slip, and when he insists upon "getting through" the traffic with an inch or so to spare on either side, he does not seem to reckon with the chances of a sudden skid. In such a case, if anyone were injured, he would undoubtedly be to blame. The side-slip itself cannot be absolfitely prevented; but he has no right to imperir the lives of other users of the road by taking what are really hazardous risks in traffic. He knows the possibilities—and the limitations —of the engine he controls, and, knowing them, it is sheer foolhardiness to assume the absence of the danger which he knows is always present.

The various routes to be followed by the competitor 3 in the forthcoming Small Car Trials have now been published, subj Pot to revision. Headquarters will be at Hereford, whence the daily trips will be made. Worcester, via Bromyard ; Ludlow, Ledbury, via Leominster; Staunton, Kingston, and, finally, the Ross district will all be visited in turn.

There isto be a timed hill-climb, if possible, each day, although some of the gradients selected will be really severe. Cars too, may be cleaned this year with- out any loss of marks resulting for the time so spent, so long as it does not exceed the limit allowed. There seems to have been a very general outcry against the dirty appearance of the vehicles which took part in the 1000 trials last year, although there was reason for the stipulation prohibiting cleaning unless marks were for- feited. Under guise of cleaning, a fisw cycle riders in the auto-cycle trials which had then just taken place were discovered to be making small adjustments to their machines, although by the rules this was strictly barred.

There is a growing feeling amongst automobilists that at least some of the money taken by the various district councils for the registration of cars, cycles, and drivers should be expended upon the main roads, both in the matter of improving their surfaces and also in treating them with some dust-laying preparation. In London alone almost 7,000 cars have been registered, and thiF is without counting the various contributions of £3 each which have been paid for manufacturer's dis- tinguishing marks.

A writer in a recent number of a trade paper gives it as his opinion that the most inconsiderate users of the road amongst motorists themselves are the drivers of small cars. It is odd how .personal experiences differ, for the great majority certainly consider that it is the drivers of large cars who are most in fault in this matter. Too many of them rely upon their speed as a protection from future trouble, knowing, as they do, that the pace at which they dash over crowded portions of the high road is almost certain to prevent their numbers being accurately taken in the event of any accident occurring through their method of driving.

The driver of the voiturette, on the other hand, Seems to be far more considerate in his progression. He usually keeps to his proper side of the road, slows down on taking a curve or a corner, and is infinitely more thoughtful in his treatment of horse traffic, eve° though stopping a single-cylinder engine may mean for him a good deal of trouble in starting it again later on. Moreover, it is noticeable that it is most often the driver of the small car who slows up on seeing another motorist stranded in distress, and asks whether he is i° need of assistance. The drivers of the large cars seldom% seem to be inclined to render this little courtesy to fellow motorist in temporary distress.

The reason of all this is not hard to find. More oftefl than not, and particularly at week-end outings, the drivers of the high h.p. ears are the salesmen end mechanics from the works, whose one idea is to let the car go" all out "—and boast afterwards of the record run made upon that particular occasion. The drivers of voiturettes are almost always amateurs driving their own vehicles; they have known trouble themselves, end,: as sportsmen, are always ready to proffer the help vatic' another may need.

Seeing him reading the board and then glancing over the front of the house, a caretaker ran up the area- ateps, and invited him to come in and "view the Premises." Without any motive for so doing, he followed her in, and went from room to room, whilst she enlarged upon the superior advantages to be found in No. 5. "Two ladies only left yesterday," she said, "and two nicer ladies you wouldn't find in a day's march. They behaved that handsome to me for the little things I was able to do for them. I was Sorry to see them go," she said, with a snivel. "But there! bless their 'earts, they've gone to some beautiful place where the sun shines all through the winter, and that's more than it does in London, I'm sure." "Do you remember the name of the place where the sun is always shining?" said Lanyon, as he Placed a piece of coin in the ever-ready fingers. "Well, I don't rightly for the moment, sir; but as near as I can come at it, it was Pan." "Pan," repeated Lanyon ; "I never heard of such a place." " Well, sir, I may be wrong—my memory's not over good; but they left me their direction on a Paper, so as if any letters came." She took out an old purse from an under pocket, and began to fumble amongst its contents. At last she drew out half a sheet of note-paper, unwrapped it, and handed it to him. Upon it he read,,in a Clear, firm hand: "Address all letters to Queen's hotel, Cannes." Wishing the old woman a hasty "Good-day," :pnyon walked out of the house, and returned to hotel. Flinging himself down in an easy-chair, AO took out his cigar-case, chose and lighted a 4:gar, and ?T..ve himself up to thought. Robert ianyon was a man of decision. The following morning found him in the Dover express en route for Cannes. He thought nothing of the journey, so used was he to be constantly on the Move. He had, too, that happy knack of settling easily down wherever he found himself, always providing that things were "fairly comfortable," as 4e expressed it. Arriving at Cannes, he drove to the hotel, the name of which he had carefully noted, and pre- Pared himself to stay for the next two .months if necessary. And so he waited, and the days passed by and became weeks; and he grew weary of having nothing to do, but he never faltered one moment in his purpose. See that woman he wluld before Ile returned home. At length one evening he came VI, after a long day's ramble, dressed in haste—for 'le was somewhat late—and walked into the dining- -1;00112. To his surprise and infinite delight, he I°und Mrs. Crombie and P.- nline Garnett seated, calmly eating their dinner. As luck would have it, they had taken their places :iext to the one he had cecupied since first he came. Overjoyed at the °Dnortunitv this gave him, he leisurely took his

image was far too constantly before his mental vision. First, as she appeared in the theatre, and again as he last saw her on the ever memorable evening at Cannes. He would dream on half- unconsciously for some time, then 'remember that he was on a track of thought which he had decided to resist, and with a mental shake would take up books or papers and so turn his attention to other things. He did not, however, find it easy to keep clear of the forbidden ground. Upon reaching Southampton he went immediately on board, and going below began to settle all things in his state-room before the starting of the i vessel. Having arranged things ship-shape, and feeling the first thud of the engine, he went on ' deok and stood by the taff-rail as the Orma made her way down Southampton Water. Be felt wonderfully lonely, much more so than he had ever felt before when leaving England. Ho knew that he was now taking the final step in removing himself out of the path of the woman he loved. For he did love her in spite of everything. No amount of schooling had had any effect in changing his feelings towards Pauline. She was the one woman in the world for him, and yet she belonged to another. He felt that as a man of honour he had done the only thing possible. To live in Pauline's presence for any length of time and not confess his love was, for him, an impossibility. Few passengers were down for dinner that evening. The vessel havina reached the open Channel had to resist the fill force of the wind, and there was promise of a nasty night. Lanyon, being an old sailor, felt no discon.fort in the way of sea-sickness; but it was unpleasant on deck and he turned in early. He was fortunate in having a state-room to himself, as the boat was not carrying her full number of passengers. It was a wild night, but he slept comfortably, and was surprised upon waking early to find a bright; clear morning. Be rose, and was out on deck in good time He felt better already. The sea always suited him and seemed to blow the cobwebs from his brain. The sun shone brightly by ten o'clock and gave promise of a fine, if not very calm day. Gradually the passengers began to appear upon deck. Chairs were placed in all the most comfortable part the ship, and things began to look generally rwve cheerful. Lanyon was promenading slowly up kmd down the deck, taking care not to stumble aga:,..,it the long chairs in his passage to and fro. • Suddenly the ship gave a lurch and threw him • against one of the chairs in which a lady was sitting. She was wrapped up in rugs and , shawls, and being sheltered, from the wind by an umbrella, it was next to impossible to get a sight of • her. Lanyon, however, was, of necessity, most i unceremonious. By SHIRLEY RAYNARD, Author of "Under the Holly," "A Thorn in the Tyre," "The Ghost of the West Wing," &e.

with the occupant of me chair and at the right side of the umbrella.

"I beg your pardon, I'm sure," said.he, but with some confusion.

Then she turned and looked into his face when, to his surprise, he beheld Pauline. "31. r. Lanyon,' said she, somewhat stiffly, "I had no idea you were on board." - "Our meeting is an equal surprise to me," re- turned Lanyon. "I quite thought you were still at Cannes."

"We were surprised to find you had left Cannes so suddenly," she said, coldly. "I hope you reached England in time for the important business you mentioned in your note to my sister ? " "Yes, thank you," said he. "I hope your sister is well. Is she travelling with you ?"

"I expect her on deck any moment. She is not the best of sailors, and has not had an over good night." Hardly were the words out of her mouth when Mrs. Crombie appcared, and Lanyon rose to allow her to take his chair. _

"Well, this is a surprise !" said she, as she recognised Lanyon. "Whatever made you run away from Cannes in that hurry ? " lie was relieved from answering this somewhat pointed question by another great lurch on the part of the ship which sent him spinning down to the rail. When he had recovered himself, and helped Mrs. Crombie with her various rugs &c., she seemed to have forgotten that she had asked it. They talked on for some minutes, and then Lanyon resumed his promenade; but he took it now on tho other side of the deck. _

- "The fates are against me," he said to himself. "I would have kept out of that woman's way, had it been possible; but here we are thrown together, and on a boat where we must meet. I could almost have sworn I saw pleasure in her face when she first recognised me, in spite of her cold manner. Well, the only way is to be with them as little as politeness will admit of. It is my only chance of not betraying myself." Mrs. Crombie, however, being an energetic little woman, and not fond of mystery, meant to get to the bottom of this one at the earliest opportunity. She said nothing about it to her sister; but she intended to learn before many hours had elapsed what had taken Lanynn away from Cannes. He, in spite of his resolve, found himself constantly in the company of the two ladies. Mrs. Crombie, in particular, seemed always ready for a chat, and her sister did not avoid him, though she continued her somewhat reserved manner.

One day, towards the end of their first week at sea, Mrs. Crombie and Lanyon were walking up and down the deck together. They had talked of one subject and then another. Mrs. Crombie had been speaking of England, and saying how much they had enjoyed their stay there. She referred casually to the pleasant trip they had had in the South of Europe. "My sister was not at all pleased about my insisting upon our returning home now," she said. "I believe she would gradly have gone back to England for at least six months more; but I felt that I must be back. She is a perfectly free agent; but I—well, perhaps Pauline has told you, Mr. Lanyon, that I am to be married directly we arrive at home."

" You ? " exclaimed Lanyon, with surprise. "I beg your pardon, Mrs. Crombie. I did not mean to imply that there was anything extraordinary in that; but I understood it was your sister who was engaged to be married." _

"Pauline," said Mrs. Crombie, laughing. "No, I am afraid there is no man suitable for her—at least, she fails to find one to her liking amongst her many admirers." Shortly afterwards she went below, smiling to herself. She knew she had discovered the secret of Lanyon's hasty retreat from Cannes. The evening was closing in, and Pauline, who had been reading on deck for some time, shut her book as the light failed her. Mrs. Crombie, who felt the cold, had not returned; and Pauline was preparing' to go below when she found Lanyon standing beside her. "You look colfl, Miss Garnett," said he, taking her rugs from her. "You had better walk for ten minutes before going down." Pauline hesitated for a moment, and then, as she did really feel chilly, decided that walking was the best thing to do. They paced up and down for some time in the gathering twilight, and then went astern to watch the track on the water left by the course of the vessel. _ _

- "Miss Garnett, I want you to know why I left Cannes," said Lanyon, turning to look into rauline's face. _ _ _

_ " You told us at the time, if I remember rightly," saiA Pauline, coldly. "I'm afraid I told you what was not exactly the truth," said he. "I left because I heard that you were engaged to be married; and I could not' bear to look at you, and think of .you as belonging to another man."

Light began to dawn upon Pauline, and joy welled up in her aching heart. "Pauline, I love you dearly," said he, tenderly. "Will you be my wife ? " Pauline's answer did not consist of many words; but it seemed perfectly satisfactory to Lanyon, short as it was, for he folded her in his arms, there in the darkness, and pressed kiss after kiss upon her lovely lips. _

[THE END.]

BANK HOLIDAY AFTERMATH.

Pawnbrokers and their Customers.

A corresPondent of the "Daily News" visited several pawnbrokers in the poorer parts of London and ascertained that Bank Holiday had depleted the savings of the people to an alarming extent, with the result that the pawnbrokers were busier than ever. In South London one of them said that he had dealt with 225 clients from the time he opened on Tuesday at 9 o'clock until 12.30. Of thearticles pledged, Sunday clothes came easily first. The 225 pledges mentioned included :- 54 Sunday suits. 10 Bicycles. 53 Pairs of trousers.

41 Coats and waistcoats (together). 18 Coats. 16 Watches. 10 Rings. The remainder of the pledges consisted of articles of household linen and furniture. Inquiries further afield showed that even in a thickly populated district 225 is an extraordinarily large num- ber of clients to receive after a Bank Holiday. At Brentford, for instance, which is a busy centre,one pawnbroker had only 84 " visitors " up till three o'clock; but," he said," I shall be busy enough this evening, for what money my class of customers have will begone by then." / Great ingenGity was shown in order to persuade the pawnbroker to increase his loan. One man of respect- able appearance earnestly begged for 15s. onhis watch, because (he said) he had received some good information about a horse for a certain race,and he wanted to "pill. on 7s. 6d. each way." The horse he mentioned, it may be remarked, ran nowhere." Pleas came from those who had friends locked up for indiscretions overnight. "I know he'll be lined ten bob by the old man," said a buxom wife at Chisvvick, " and if you don't lend me seven and a tanner on this (a substantial-looking clock) I can't make it up." The excuses for pawning goods were summed up by the various proprietors thus : To pay fines for being drunk. To continue, the Bank Iloliday.for another day. To back " certs."

To procure liveners." To square an aquaintance for " bashing " him over- night and avoiding the police court. To repay debts incurred on Monday—chiefly cards. To carry on until next pay-day.

COTTON OPERATIVES SAVINGS. Affected by Short Time.

Short time has been in operation at Oldham for ten months, affecting about 30,000 operatives, and the total loss in wages has averaged about £lO,OOO per week. It has been-arranged that short time, which began in June last year, shall continue to the end of the present month, when the total loss in wages from that cause will be £500,000, or about as. Bd. per operative per week. The reduction in the wages bill of an average mill, owing to short time, does not exceed £BO a week. The condition of the trade during the past twelve months has undoubtealv had an effect on the savings clubs, and it is expected that the disbursements at Oldham Wakes, which begin on the last Saturaay in August. will be some thousands of pounds less than those of former years. As an illustration, it may be pointed out that the wakes of Royton, in the Oldham Parliamentary borough commence this week-end, and the total sum paid out from the savings clubs has been £9,000, against nearly £12,000 last August.

FOREIGN VIEW OF THE LANCASHIRE OPERATIVE.

Continental textile papers this week devote consider- able attention to the holiday funds raised in Lancashire. "Undoubtedly the cotton operatives in the weaving and spinning, and kindred industries," says the report, "are the aristocracy of labour. They have realised for some time that it is necessary not only for the iron but for the human machine to be from time to time oiled in order to do its work well. When will the Austrian textile workers beable to have such holidays?"

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

Stockton Heath and Warrington Light

Railvi ays.

Sir,—l am not at all surprised that Mr. Westbrook is anxious to hate a rest from his labours of the past few weeks. A mental diet of law,seilkand ghosts, on which he feasted during his last effusion, is enosigh to upset anyone, and that he was mentally upset was shown by the tone he adopted. 1. hope, however, that while he is recruiting he will consider whether it would not be -wiser in future to drop the policy of obstruction, of -which he has been such a strenuous advocate in the past, and adopt in its place a progressive one, so that some- thing tangible may be achieved. To "grasp at shadows and miss thesnbstance" is a weary task and this the advocates of obstructien in Stockton Heath, much to their chagrin and sorrow, have found out, for in "grasp- ing at the shadow of a reduction of 3d. in gas in exchange for the substance of electric light," theyhave lost both.—Yonrs truly,

E. ENGLAND.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
A Neglected Antiquity.

Si r,—l have SOM9 little respect for "ancient land- I arks" or places of historic interest. Two yell-a ago I ook a pilgrimage to "St. Ossvald's Well" at Winwick, having as a companion, a Yorkshireantiquarian. I need hardly say we found the surroundings of this old relic, very filthy indeed, and in a seriously neglected condi- tion. Verbal references to this state of affairs appear to have had no good effect, as our 'visit last Satur- day shows. I therefore beg to enclose copy of a resolution unani- monsly adopted by the party of "Guilders," and trust this public notice may have the designed and desired effect. I should also like to learn from some one of your readers who may be "in the know" how or when the public footpath leading to "Prince Rupert's Well' has been closed.—Thanking you in anticipation sincerely yours,

GEORGE CLARKE.

46, King-street, Earlestown.

_ [COPY OF RESOLUTION.] We, members of the Earlestown "Wesley Guild," do hereby express our deep regret to find this once noted and historic shrine "St. Oswald's Well " in such a sad state of neglect, and trust this public notice may lead 'to some local authority giving that care and attention i to this once sacred spot, it s certainly entitled to receive.—On behalf of the members, Signed GEORGE CLARKE (Conductor.)

An Illegal Birching. Sir,—Replying to Sir W. Foster on July 21st, the Home Secretary admitted that a boy n 'mod Heekes was •recently birched by the police—on their own sole responsibility—as an alternative to being charged with theft. "The action of the police superintendent in birching the boy," said Mr. Akers-Douglas, "was clearly most improper, and I am glad to find that the Chief Constable takes a very serious view of his misconduct." We too, are extremely glad to learn that the Home Secretary is at last awaking to the fact that these "extra-judicial" birchings (illegal we should prefer to call them) are a very serious matter indeed, and none the less so because the pretence is always made that the boy is flogged in ordet to save him from imprisonment. But is it, afterall, very surprising that the police should new take upon themselves to birch boys under this flimsy pretence of humanity, when they have for several years seen magistrates not only evading the law in the same manner, but allowed to do so by the Home Office? We have again and again drawn the Home Secretary's attention to cases where magistrats have grossly exceeded their powers by cajoling the parents of youthful culprits into allowing them to be birched for offences which are not legally liable to that punish- ment; but Hr. Akers-Douglas has done his utmost to screen and abet this illegality. He is now discovering that illegality, when once tolerated in high places, has a way of spreading downwards; and we have this scandal- ous instance of a police superintendent—in reliance upon the example set by magistrates and condoned by the Home Oflice—infliciing corporal punishment at his own sweet will. We are getting on !—r am,yours faithfully, JOSEPH COLLINSON. Humanitarian League, 53, Chancery-lane, London, 'August, 1904.

Rev. H. Dunnico and His Critics. • With your permission I will in accordance with my promise continue my letter, the former part of which appeared in last week's issue. So many and varied have been the subjects totiched upon in this controversy that the primary and original question "The aims and objects of the Social Service League" has almost been lost to sight. In lieu of that, your readers have been treated to an exuberant outburst of fictitious facts and fabricated doctrines upon which thelndependent Labour Party is supposed to be based. If the I.L.P. is all that its adherents claim it to be,then I for one cannot under- stand its blind and unreasoning fury against all political parties other than its own and against all persons who are unableto utter its particular "shibboleth." Such bigoted hostility can have but one result, it will alienate the sympathy and help of those whose co-operation, it, above all parties, can ill afford to ignore and it will obscure,even if it does not fatally destroy,the truth and patriotiem I am not unwilling to admit it possesses in no small degree. Now" P. 8." plies me with so many questions of one sort or another and with such audacity that I am some- what perplexed as to her motive in asking them. More- over one looks in vain for the smallest element of that virtue always associated with the sex to which she 'belongs. These people Mr. Editor are turning the world upside 'down. Only last week an unattached "hanger on" had the audacity to question your "sublime -editorial consideration" and now a fully fledged member •of this militant party has ridden rough-shod over all those supernatural prerogatives identified for centuries with the "profession" of . which I happen to be a humble member,and not only has she captured the "confessional box" but also she has instabled herself "Father—T beg your pardon "—"Mother confessor" and has taken to 'herself the right to probe the secrets of the human mind. As a rule I have not any aversion to answering ques- tions, but your correspondent persists in asking them after the fashion of Sergeant Bnzfuz as though she expected to get her witness committed for contempt of court if he should decline to answer questions which in his opinion are either irrelevant, ridiculous or insolent. For instance take such a question as the following as a type of the rediculous. In her first letter "P. 8. writes as follows—"I have heard the Rev. H. Minnie.) and some of his supporters declare themselves to be Socialists, he himself taking active part in their meetings. This being so, why do we find him working strenuously in the Liberal cause? Is it possible for a man to be a true Liberal and a true Socialist at one and the same time ?" The profundity of such a question is appalling. However, let us con- sider it. In the first place, what is Socialism ? Clear ideas are always necessary to intelligent discussion, but clear ideas are considered trivial matters by correspon- dents" who have a deep sense of the sacredness of their cause." "P. 8. uses the words "Socialist" and "Socialism" as though they conveyed the simplest of ideas, but notwithstanding her glibness, I challenge her, as I have challenged her in a previous letter to-define •the terms, and until she does so it would be wiser on her part, to eliminate the phrase "shirking the point " from future fulminations. It has not been possible even by the aid of long dissertations to discover even the paternity of the word; still less has any sacceeded in fixing the exact signification of the t rms. But sir, is not every man who gives his attention to social questions and desires the ameliora- tion of snciety,at liberty to call himself a Socialist? In my opinion he is, and even" P. B. " darenot say that he is not. Now will your consistent and logical and broad minded correspondent point out wherein a Social- ist is prevented from allying himself with any party, which in his honest opinian is seeking to promote that end. I await her answer and perhaps she will answer her own question for the benefit of yolir readers. Take another question quite in keep- ing with the rest.. In another letter " P. 13." states "I see no necessity for Mr. Dunnico to qualify himself to be what everyone else took him to be—' a Christian Socialist '—but does Mr. Dunnico infer that Socialists are not Christians; if so, he is greatly mistaken in snits of his logic." Now a few weeks ago I asked "P. 8. to give a definition of Socialism as advocated by the I.L.P. (and it must net be forgotten that it is Inde- pendent Labour Party Socialism we are now discussing). She declined with that becoming modesty so typical of 'inability, but recommended ine to put the ques- tion to "their lecturers and chairman." Well, the question was put to Councillor Stott, and as he is considered capable of representing the party in our Town Council, I assume he is capable of stating their belief. Well, Councillor Stott deliber- ately stated in the presence of a large crowd that " Jesus Christ was not a Socialist awl that the Socialism advocated and taught by the T.L.P. is not based upon the teachings of Jesus." Could lac. have stated more clearly that the Socialism of Jesus is not the Socialism of the 1.L.P.? "P.B. 's" patter about Christian and non- Christian Socialism is mere cant, and reveals in the most striking manner that either Councillor Stott or "P.B."is ignorant of the principles of the party they pro- fess to below,* to. But that is a matter they will prob- ably be able to settle amongst themselves at their "meetings held each Monday night in Corporation-st." I regret Mr. Editor that I am compelled to omit much that I had intended saying, but in the course of a week or so I may perhaps be allowed to give further evidence in support of every state-sent and accusation I have made. Tn the meantime T commend the two questions raised as being worthy of a straight answer and I would also suggest that it would be as well if these eminent, political guides were to ascertain what they really do believe before bewildering our minds with their dis- cordant and contradictory statementa.—Very sincerely yours, HERBERT DUNNICO. Shrewsbury, Shropshire.

38, Bewsey-street, Warrington.

Sir,—Once again and for the last time I will trouble you to allow me to reply to the Rev. H. Dunnico. Were it not that so many are watching the fray between the great somebody— and "little nobodies "I would not answer such a contemptible piece of personal abuse. I am deeply sorry that these letters have taken the form they have. They were intended to be a debate pure and simple on progressive principles—either Liberal, Socialistic or versus the two, but owing to the shirking and cowardly tactics of one of the writers they have resolved into, oa the one side, personal abuse, and on the other, defence, and now, retaliation. To begin with Mr. Dunnico would not be justified in publishing full names Of your correspondents, and what he means by "boasting of their dishonest methods in certain circles" I do not know. Several friends have asked me how Mr. Dunnico came by his informa- tion, and not unnaturally conclude it to be through the paper. Such, however, would be a breach of confidence on the wirt of the paper and I for one know too well the integrity of a The Examiner" and its Editor to doubt them on such points. Mr. Dunnico obtained his information through the confidence of a mutual friend on the appearance of my first letter and which confi- dence he has now treacherously made use of, and at the same time stabbed our friend in the back. Why should Mr. Dunnico publish the name? How dare he violate the right of personal desire to remain anonymous? It is easy to see that Mr. Dunnico's estimation of merit depends upon sex and identity. I desired to meet, and be met on common ground, fully preparing myself for what consequences might accrue, provided my opponent would stick to truth and points under discussion—Points which he has very carefully shirked. Was mine not the right that that desire should be respected, without its being tampered with by your correspondent? Arrogance indeed! Who arrogated to Mr. Minnie() the right to dict.tte what T should, or should not disolose ? I used a nom de plume for reasons above given. Mr. Dunnico has acted in this matter in a mean and cowardly manner—using abusive means to cover a miser- able failure, and to excuse his own hypocritically faise position. I have not claimed "A.A." as my friend, I only wish I had the honour of "A.A.'s" friendship, or of even know- ing who "A.A." is, which I do not, but my curiosity is not degraded enough to seek the information from his friends and use it to lowerhim in public estimation. We do not appear under at least a dozen "noms de plume". Nor do we take an unfair advantage. Mr. Dunnico publicly challenged three men from a party of uneducated (compared with him) working-men, to meet him in debate. It was not taken up, but now that three—curious co-incidence—have undesignedly met him in straightforward argument, his cry goes up "unfair advantage." And so it happens that the great somebody cannot even meet three "little nobodies" in honest controversy, but must needs resort to stigma and abuse. Referring to "A. A.'s" discipleship of Marie Corelli, Mr. Dunnico has evidently read Marie Corelli. " *A." has done no more. Therefore Mr. Dunnico is equally as much a follower of that distinguished writer as is "A. A.," but with this difference between them, that whereas "A. A."—not a particularly professing Christian culls the best and the beautiful, the reverend gentleman, the paid exponent of Christianity, the earthly represen- tative of Christ to his flock, remembers the bad, hiding his difficulty under meaningless generalisation. It is intensely refreshing and indeed one cannot help but admire the " Plazatorian " ingenuity with which Mr. Dunnico backs out of his difficulties, Political and otherwise, and that grandiloquent air of his with which he triumphantly "takes his place at the fore 0 "—but if he is a somebody he will find that even "little nobodies" quite see through, but won't have his bluff.—Truly he is very very funny—but young yet—Thanking you for all favours.—Yours sincerely, BEATRICE L. PEMBERTON, 64, St. Mary's-street, Warrington.

Sir,—Under the above heading Mr. Dunnico last week contributed a "brilliant letter.' His consistency is some- thing " remarkable," his sarcasm is "delicious," his language "gentlemanly." If the truth must be told the ' reverend gentleman is rather an irresponsible person. He is so mixed up with one thing and another that he loses his head. Mr. Dunnico in public lam afraid is ' more or less a firebrand. Mr. Dunnico suffers from that terrible thine'—a Nonconformist conscience. It makes him a Christian, Socialist, Liberal, Passive Re- sister, Social Service Leaguer all rolled into one. Mr. Dunnico is also an uncertain quantity as a temperance L reformer. : And now with reference to Mr. Dunnico's treatment of his critics. He gabbles off a stock reply to trouble- some correspondents, he says things which are not entirely true and enlarges upon trivial matters and I practically turns them into lies. For instance he says 1 that within the last few months I have written under L at least six different " noms de plume." That's a lie and Mr. Minnie() knoars it only too well. I have done very little writing for some time now,except of course during this last week or two and previous to Mr. Dunnico's last letter I had only signed myself under two : assumed names " Onelnterested " and "Anonymous." I used the latter because in one week's issue of this paper 'I had another letter signed "One Interested." Perhaps Mr. Dunnico does not remember advising me, at least for some time to write, under a " nom de plume" and • perhaps he does not remember asking me to write my ; first letter on the Social Brotherhood Idea in reply to L " Free Critic." I noticed also last week Mr. Dnnnico referred to one ' of the correspondents as "her." Now I informed Mr. Dunnico, in confidence. I presnme,that one of the corres- pondents was a lady and I say unhesitatingly that he had no right to use that information in any way whatever. Since however he has made use of it. I may say that when I told him and speaking about "her" he said that he would give "her" a good dressing down. Evidently he has been trying to keep his word judging from the tone of his letters in reply to "P. 8. Strong Christian adjectives are a feature of Mr. Dunnico's letters. I now come to something that personally concerns myself. I presume Mr. Dunnico refers to me when he speaks of low music halls and that I may "be frequently found there."Doee Mr.Dunnico mean the Scotland-road music hall. If he does and says that I may be frequently found there, well then, all that I can say is the Rev. H. Dunnico is a liar—no more and no less. admit willingly that I have been there one week, perhaps even two weeks, but no more. But this is no secret,f or I told Mr. Dunnico himself that I had been,but I explained to him it was more to see what alterations had been madeland to judge as to the class of artistes that appeared, and as to the steniard of the performance given. I may say here that if the weekly performances areup to the standard of the one T witnessed there's not much harm done. But as to being found there fre- quently I deny it. Perhaps Mr. Dunnico means the Royal Court Theatre. This place also T only visit two or three times in the year, and that only when there is something pretty good to see. Neither the Royal Court Theatre nor the Scotland- I road Music Hall are very low in the first plaee. Some lof those who go there may be low parsons but that has nothing to do with me. There are others who attend who are not "low." , I don't apologise to Mr. Dunnico for attending a theatre; I shall go when and as ofken as I please. There , lire only two times I regret going and on those two , ?fccasione my reverend friend was preaching there, giving ' vent to his usual humbug. Low as I am, however, Mr. ' Minnie° has admitted that "One Interested" worte "two interesting letters" on the Social Brotherhoed idea and with much that T said he was in entire agree- ment. Not so had am ? Low and degraded as I am I was not too low to be Dunnico's friend, and to have shelter under his roof " as a friend." And aeatin what do your readers think of this? A Sunday or two ago his chapel wanted someone to give a paper to fill in a Sunday afternoon. To cut the story short"( gave that paper with Mr. Dunnico's know- ledee and consent. The low music hall attender is Invited, and gives a paper in Mr. Dunnico's own church and Mr. Dunnico raises no objection. Truly it is wonderful—l cannot resist a smile at the reverend gentleman's remarkable consistency. And remember this. Mr. Dunnico know I had been once to the Scotland. road music hall, bec else I had told him so. Mr. Dunnico asked me in one of his letters a week or two ago to excuse "an extremely tired man." If he means that he was suffering from 'swelled head "I do excuse him. Perhaps Mr. Dunnico thisking that I would not reply

but don't do it again." Your neighbour's house may be 1 on fire, and the flames may be threatening you, but you must not lend a hand, or fetch a bucket of water, with- out the consent of the executive. The agricultural labourer may perish from the land, and you must not tryto save him without the permission of men who know less about these things than you know. When Czars and Sultans play these games we call it " tyranny " and " oppression." When working men play these tricks on their fellow workmen they call it " discipline" and "unity." They can call it what they like, but it is the same hateful thing everywhere, and I would rather be under the Czar of Russia, or the Sultan of Turkey, than at the mercy of a Vrant in clogs, God help any poor wretch who is under the heel or an ignorant, unfeeling despot, who is permitted to rule before he has learned to serve, and who knows infinitely more than the "boss" who drives him. And if there is anything in this world more hate- ful than a tyrant, it is a committee of tyrants, for you .an get rid of oae despot a good deal easier than you can get rid of a dozen, or a score of them. The worst feature of this kind of tyranny is that it grows on what it feeds upon, and "as the old cock crows, the young one learns." The latest sample of this depotism is the case mentioned by James Sexton. He tells us that the operative stonemasons claim the right to muzzle any man who comes into theirtown, or village at any political meeting, unless he first obtains their consent. So if a man wants to speak te them ho must first get the consent of the executive and then of the local committee. Men who are so dreadfully afraid of truth that they dare not listen to it are certainly not fit to be leaders of men.

" Ylabon," the popular Welsh miners' representatioe, says that "if Labour cannot run a candidate of its own it should support the candidate who comes nearest its own ideals." But this doctrine of common sense and worldly wisdom is rank blasphemy in the eyes of certain men belonging to the Labour Representation Committee. Their game is to make Labour members of Parliament into political Ishmaelites, "whose hands shall be against every man, and every man's hand against them."

If that is their_ programme they will have to be

fought and resisted io he bitter end. I love the cause of Labour, but I love freeslom more, and I have no desire to see the Labour movement captured by men who have axes of their own to grind and interests of their own to serve. The muzzling order of the Labour Representation Committee will keep out men like John Burns, and Richard Bell, and William Abraham, and every other Labour leader who has a spark of indepen- dence in him, and it will let in dummies and puppets who will move when the committee pulls the strings. John Burns says that "Labour cannot afford to quarrel with its best friends," so he is treated by some , as a heathen and an outcast,though he has probably done as much for the benefit of the working man as any dozen of his opponents. I take my stand beside honest John Burns and William Abraham, and I will do all I can to promote the best interests of the working man. But lam taking none of his arrogance, and impudence, and tyranny. The Labour movement is on its trial, and its future will largely de- pend on how it behaves itself. If it is going to spend all its energies in prAvoking quarrels and resisting de- sirable reforms, and fighting men who have spent their lives in freedom's cause, it will not have its troubles to seek. It sorely needs wise leaders who are strong enough to lead,and will not be "pinpricked" and pushed from behind. Tyranny is the same hateful,cursed thing to me everywhere and always, and wherever I meet it I shall fight it and resist it to the death.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
rALL RIGHTS RESERVED.I

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour and Social Problems."

MUZZLING THE LABOUR MEMBERS.

"The operative stonemasons are seeking to prevent members of the Parliamentary Committee from speak- ing at any political meeting without first having consulted the Trades Council in the locality, and received their gracious consent. This is the tail wagging the dog with a vengeance."—JAmEs SEXTON.

The Labour Representation Committee some time ago decided that no member of Parliament under its control shall be allowed to take part in any contested election, or speak on behalf of any Parliamentary candi- date during the contest, without first obtaining the permission of the executive. Now it is one thing to frame such a rule as this for grown-up men with brains and wills of their own' and it is another thing to enforce it. As a mltter of fact this rule has been dodged, and evaded, or ignored, and I am not going to be hard on the men who have broken it. But the executive will stand no nonsense about it, and the offenders have been compelled to answer for their sins, and they have been told in pretty plain Eng- lish tivit they must not do it again. This rule may be wise, or it may be foolish, but its consequences will be so very serious to the cause of labour, that I feel bound to criticise it, and examine it for myself. I.—WHY SHOULD LABOUR IGNORE THE CLAIMS OF THE LABOURER?

At contested elections, when candidates are soliciting the working man's vote, and asking him to support all sorts of measures that he does not understand, why should the men who profess to be his leaders refuse to advise him, and instruct him, and help him out of his difficulties? The Labour Representation Committee has for its object the election of working men to Parliament, and the formation of an independent labour party, and it shows its independence by refusing to help the cause of labour anywhere, unless it has a seat to capture, or an axe of its own to grind. We have had quite a number of bee-elections lately, in which the agricultural labourer has been the pre- dominating partner, and his vote was strong enough to capture the seat if he had only been educated, and organised. I have visited scores of villages in the Market Harborough and Oswestry Divisions, where the tiller of the soil is being ruthlessly rooted up and exterminated. He is not allowed to have any allotment land, unless he will pay twice as much rent for it as the farmer is paying. The housing problem is crushing the life out of him because the farmers monopolise all the Cottages, and if hlkdoes not serve them he can leave the place. If he is an agitator, and tries to organise his fellow labourers, he is a marked man, and he must go. So he leaves the village, and tramps into the town to compete with thousands more of our unemployed in our overstocked labour market. In a small town in Shropshire the population used to be about seven thousand, and now itis less than two thousand, and the miserable 'work of rural depopu- lation is going On still. I have been advising the agricultural labourers in those villages to vote for any candidate who will support a drastic reform in our land laws, and make the provision of allotments and work- men's dwellings compulsory, instead of permissive. I urged them to support any man who is willing to give them the benefit of the Workmen's Compensation Act, and protect trade union funds, and give equal rights all round both to masters and workmen.

Now I want you t ) realise that these are not questions of party politics, because some of these reforms are advocated by all parties in the state. But they are bread-and-butter policies to the agricultural labourers and rural artisans, and they are burning questions to every trades unionist in the kingdom. If we could reform our land laws we could transform our villages and relieve our congested labour markets in the towns. Surely the time has come when we can deal with these bread-and-butter questions without flinging each other :into the dirty ditch of party politics. If the Labour leaders would give more time and thought to these things, and spend less time on mutual jealousies, and trying to curtail each other's liberties, we could help the cause of freedom materially.

-- -- Instead of abstaining from all bye-elections in which they are not running a candidate of their own, they should send down one or two of their best men to every such election. They should authorise their representa- tives to draw up a list of the measures in which they are interested, and interview both candidates, and then fight and work for the man who will pledge himself to do most for the cause of Labour. _ _

The leaders of the Irish party send down representa- tives to every bye-election where the Irish vote is a power to be reckoned with. The United Kingdom Alliance sends agents to organise and concentrate the temperance voters in favour of licensing reform. The Free Traders and the Protectionists placard the towns, and hold meetings every day to advertise their opinions. But the Labour Representation Committee cannot be trusted to do anything half so reasottable and sensible i as this. If its object sto voice the grievances of the worker, and ameliorate his condition, it ought either to send to every bye-election some of its best leaders to fight their cause, or it should allow them to use their own judgment, and go as private individuals. But it does neither, and this dog-in-the-manper policy is re- sponsible for the loss of ever so many seats that might have been won for progress and social reform. I felt this very keenly during the Oswestry campaign. We won that great victory entirely by the agricultural labourer's vote,and some of us addressed three and four meetings every day to educate and organise these voters, and yet the so-called labour leaders ware conspicuous by their absence. They never lifted a finger, or raised a voice to help us, and yet they will crow over it as victory for labour. But they had no hand in it, and if their policy had been adopted by every progressive, the victory never would have been won. lI.—I OBJECT TO THE DICTATION OF THE LABOUR REPRESENTATION COMMITTEE ON

MATTERS OF CONSCIENCE. It is a mutual admiration society that thinks no small beer of itself, but it presumes sadly too much when it muzzles its representatives, and forbids them to speak at elections' when important labour questions have to be thrashed out betweea candidates and electors. I do not know who its members are, and I do not ware, for I am not fighting men' but eternal principles. No man has any business to take away my right of private judgment, or interfere between me and my conscience, or dictate to me what .I shall do in all cir- cumstances. If I see a wrong that needs righting, or a crooked thing that must be put straight,l shall ask no- body's leave whether I shall do it, or leave it undone. Let us see how this rule works.

_ During the Market Harborongh election I mat several well-known members of Parliament, who might, and ought to have, rendered yeoman service to the cause of Labour. But they were under an insane maziding order that prevented them from appearing on the same platform as the man who advocated all the measures in their own programme. Mr. Bell went to the railway works at Wigston, and snoke as a railwayman. Mr. Shackleton and Mr. Henderson went to Leicester, and addressed either a Band of Hope, or a temperance meeting, while I was there. How these three gentlemen could talk on these questions during election times, and keep politics out, is a mystery to me. One thing is certain, they observed the letter of *he 'law, if not its spirit, for they did not appear on any platform in the company of the candidate who advocated their bread-and-butter policy, though they were heartily in sympathy with it. I chaffed one or two of these muzzled members, and asked them if their task masters had given them per- mission to come into Leicestershire during a contested election. They took my banter and jokes as cheerfully as they could, but they evidently did not care to be looked on as tied-house politicians, who had to take their orders from inferior men, and pocket some insults into the bargain. The following week some of them paid a flying visit to the Davenport election, and when this came to the ears of their " bosses " the fat was in the flre. The Labour Representation Committee met in London, and passed a resolution to the effect that having heard ,the explanations made by Messrs. Shackleton and Henderson, M.P.'s, as to their appearance in the constituencies of Market Hae)nrough and Devonport, at the recent bye elections therein, namely, that they did not appear under political auspices, nor attend any meeting, but went solely in the interests of the Labour movement, accepted the same, hut, inasmuch as such appearances during elections afforded oppor- tunities to the press to misrepresent, and thereby weakened the Labour Representation Comnaitttee move- ment, declared that in future the executive must decide what action, if any, should be taken at bye-elections. Now I take it that this is a verdict of " Not guilty—

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE PASSIVE RESISTERS AT STOCKTON HEATH.— The date of the sale of the,watches seized for the balance of the poor rate which includes a portion set apart for educational purpoves,has not yet been fixed. The reason for the delay is not yet apparent, but we may say that one of the passive resisters is the Rev. H. Dunnico, who happens to be away on his holidays. It appears clear, however, that the reverend gentlemen are not only at present minus their watches, but also their votes.

PICNIC TO CHESTER.—A successful picnic to Chester organized by Mr. J. Hallows, took place on Bank Holiday. A party of sixty left Victoria-square in omnibuses at eight o'clock, and on arriving at the ancient city,partaok of dmner at Mr. Fletcher's dining rooms, Brook-street,. Afterwards various places of interest were viewed. Some of the visitors went through the Cathedral and saw the tablet recently erected in memory of the Cheshire soldiers who fell in South Africa. Others found pleasure in boating on the Dee, and Eccleston Perry was a popular resort. - At five o'clock tea was served, and votes of thanks were accorded to Messrs. Hallows and Fletcher. The return journey was commenced shortly after six o'clock, Stockton Heath being reached without mishap at about ten.

MEETING OF THE PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE

A meeting of the Parochial Committee was held in the National Schools on Thursday evening, Mr. G. Pilling presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. F. Warburton, J. Whitley, J. W. Bouchier, F. Pearson, J. Mounfleld, W. Fairhurst, J. Hallows, T. R. Westbrook, W. Mclntyre, W. E. Hough facting clerk), J. Swinton (sorveyor), and J. Farrington (inspector of nuisances.)

===== 0002601_19040806_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
SANITARY MATTERS.

Mr. Whitley moved that a Sinitaa:y Commlto° be appointed, consisting of Messrs. Clarke, Warburton, Mclntyre, Williams, Welsby and T. R. Westbrook. He selected those gentlemen because they were members of only one committee, and the duties of the Parochial Committee would be better divided by their appoint- ment.

--The Acting Clerk said Mr. Clarke had expressed the opinion that the Sanitary Committee should consist of the whole of the members of the Parochial Committee. Mr. Mciunfield seconded the motion. Mr. Pearson proposed that the whole of the members of the Parochial Committee be constituted a special sanitary committee, and hold special meetings for the consideration of sanitary matters, the first meeting to be held that day fortnight. He did not particularly object to Mr. Whitley 's motion, although it did not specify the duties of the committee. His object in moving the amendment 'WM chiefly that the whole com- mittee might go into sanitary business in greater detail. Th e parish was enlarging very rapidly and in a few years would have doubled itself. At present it had a large population, and it was necessary that there should be adequate sanitary appliances. The time had come when they should review their sanitary business in several branches. They ought to consider whether it would pay them to undertake the disposal of their sewage. The payments to the special contractor who did the work were increasing yearly, and he thought the time would soon arrive when the committee could do it themselves. On the other hand, it might be worth while for the Warrington Corporation to undertake the business for the committee at an arranged payment. They were certain that if the Corporation did the work with the appliances at their disposal it would be done well, and the committee would have no recurrence of the frequent complaints they had had lately regarding sanitation. If the committee was formed he intended to submit several resolutions at its first meeting. One would be that metal pails be substituted gradually for the present wooden ones. If Mr. Whitley had proposed that six gentlemen be appointed a committee of inspec- tion to report to the Sanitary Committee, he (Mr. Pearson) would have sympathised with the idea. Mr. Whitley said he was willing to withdraw his motion. He was very glad one man at least was getting converted to the impression that the parish required a sanitary committee. The reason he did not define the duties was that he had been told he could not do so until the committee had been actually appointed. The chairman had allowed Mr. Pearson a little latitude in that direction.

The Chairman: No. He has not defined any duties. Mr. Bouchier seconded the amendment. He pointed out that ever since he became connected with the Parochial Committee many sanitary questions had been discussed.

Mr. Whitley's motion having been withdrawn, the amendment was unanimously agreed to.
