VOLUNTEERS IN GAM?.

THE SECOND WEEK AT PERHAM DOWN.

[BY OUR OWN CORRESPONDENT.] Perham Down,

SUNDA.Y.

This morning the camp was early astir, and the IJcal I battalion paraded at 7.30 for a church parade. The weather is now all that can be desired, although the , heat at times is very oppressive. The service was taken by the chaplain (Rev. W. Bracecamp) who delivered a stirring address. Mr. Bracecamp seems to be thoroughly enjoying his stay under canvas, and is frequently to be seen talking to the men in their lines. This afternoon there was an excursion to Southampton and the Isle of Wight and most of the mea took advantage of the reduced fares to these places of interest. Those who , went round the Island had the pleasure of a view of the flagship " Victory " immortalised in prose and verse. Most of the Castle liners were to be seen in the Solent and al.o his Majesty's yacht. The volunteers had been waned not to demonstrate as they passed the yacht, but so close did their vessel go that the Queen waved her hand, and at once a spontaneous burst of cheering came from the officers and men on board. A large number of men went te Andover, Marlborough, Salisbury, and Stonehenge and thoroughly enjoyed themselves.

MONDAY. The weather was again very warm and a big day's work was anticipated. At 6.30., the companies were out practising semaphore signalling under company arrange- ments. After breakfast, the battalion were out in shirt sleeves and took part in attack practice and outpost duty in "A" area, and some very good work was done. Rifle inspection was the order of the afternoon. The cyclists under Captain Haslam went for a long ride and came back smothered in dust. The route taken was through

Everleigh, Amesbury, Ifuiford, Pa-rkhouse, ShiptOn, Tidworth and home, a distance of about 50 miles. The scouts, in particular, did some very good work. The brigade signallers were out on station duty in the vicinity of camp. In the evening, the regimental sports were commenced and the three-legged race caused much amusement, especially when two pairs of com- petitors ran full, tilt into the chaplain and much dis- turbed that gentleman's equilibrium.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
TUFSDAY.

Again very warm, and there was no parade before breakfast. At 8.30., the battalion went out in shirt sleeves and practised advanced and rear-guard tactics. In the evening, a shooting match was arranged between the officers and sergeants of the battalion. Some capital marksmanship was witnessed and the officers triumphed in the end._

===== 0002601_19040806_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY.

To-day,the operations were on a very extensive scale. The idea was that a red (western) force had reached. Ludgershall this morning and a blue force was dis- patched to recapture the place. The boundaries of th• area were :—North, Tidworth school and a road leading through Perham Down • south and east, Government boundaries ; west, Rabbit Hill. In these operations the Cheshires were opposed to the other three battalions in camp. After some skirmishing, a charge engagement took place on a hill oye/looking, Tidworth. Captain Linaker, of the Cheshires, earned great praise for an admirable defence of a wood against three tiasse his number of men. The maxim of the Cheshires wa• in a

fine position, and fairly swept the ranks of the enemy. The Duke of Connaught witnessed the operations, and I also saw the Rev. W. Bracecamp enjoying himself trotting about on his "charger." Captains H. G. Roberts and R. Smith arrived in camp this morning and met with an enthusiastic welcome from their respective companies.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
TH 17RSDAY.

To-day was another big day for the whole of the brigade. At 7.30 the Warrington battalion paraded in shirt sleeves and marched to a position near Bulford camp, where they were opposed by the other three battalions. A very stiff fight took place on the left flank of the local battalion, and the men displayed great efficiency in keeping under 'cover. Towards 11 o'clock the weather, which had been very hot, took a sudden change for the worse, and a very severe thunderstorm was experienced. The march Nick to camp was com- menced at 1 o'clock, and camp was reached at about 2.30. The men are in good health generally, but one or two have had slight attacks of sunstroke. A change has been made in the uniform of the battalion, khaki having been substituted for the white haversacks. The valises have all been dispensed with.

THE RETURN. The men will leave for borne to-day (Saturday), and are expected to arrrve at Warrington between 8 and 9 o'clock. ,

A RAMBLE THROUGH WARGRAVE AND WINWIGK.

On Saturday the members of the Earlestown Wesley Guild enjoyeda very pleasant ramble through Wargrave and Winwick, closing with an open-air service at Buttonwood. Mr. George Clarke, Earlestown, was responsible for the arrangements, which were carried out very successfully. The party started from the Lecture Hall, Cross-lane, soon after 2 p.m, and pro- ceeded by way of Fairclough-street and Wargrave- road, and on reaching the church the peculiar style of masonry in the building of the tower was noted I At the. Vulcan Brow, New Hey Farm," once the residence of the Brothertons,*who owned much I property in the district prior to 1908,when .nost of the property passed into the hands of the Legh family was pointed out. Passing through the fields towards "Red Hank, "attention was called to the apparatus for depositing and taking up the Mail bags by express trains. Arriving at Winwick-road, the probable site of "Gallows Croft," was indicated as the party turned into Hermitage-lane, where at a convenient spot a halt was called. Whilst a rest was taken, Mr. George Clarke gave explanatory accounts of several historic places of interest :—" The Bloody Stone," near Reformatory Bridge; " Gallows Croft" and the "Battle of Red Bank" at the "Pass of Winwick", where the Royal Forces, after a runningflight of three days from Preston, made a stand and were routed by Cromwell's Ironsides, after a desperate struggle, in which over 1,000 were slain, and nearly 2,000 taken prisoners. Many of the latter had taken refuge in Winwick Church, where they were captured and disarmed. After viewing "St. Oswald's Well," at this point, the party listened to sos high popular resort when i ashot r t sv a s reputed possess to historyp (5f this once popular curative pro perties ; pilgrims coining from all parts of the country to obtain possible relief from their ailments by contact with the healing waters of St. Oswald. But whatever powers it might have possessed ages gone by, its appearance just now is anything but attractive. On the contrary it was found extremely filthy and dis- agreeable. Strong comments were made upon this sadly neglected relic of past ages, culminating in the adoption of a resolution to be published in the local papers with the view of drawing public attention to it, and bringing about possible improvement. Pro- ceeding to Hermitage Green, fine views of the surrounding country were freely admired, after which a visit was paid to "Prince Rupert's Well," but the once public footpath to this spot where the Royalist Cavalry were refreshed in Cromwellian times, was now a thing of the past and access could only be obtained through private gardens; otherwise those interested must be satisfied by a sur- vey from the roadway. Reference was made to the fact that near this place was a station where one or more of the Hermit friars of St. Augustine were located and took alms from the many pilgrims paying visits to St. Oswald's Well. The party then pro- ceeded towards Winwick, and enjoyed a' godd tea, and a further rest at Rose Cottage; afterwards visiting the church, where some historic features were pointed out and explained by Mr. George Clarke, particular references being made to the wonderful " Winwick Pig." At 6 p.m. an augmented party proceeded towards Burtonvvood where an open-air service was conducted by Mr. George Clarke,who with Mr. George E. Warburton gave addressss. Miss L. Faragher, gave sacred solos very acceptably, and Miss Eleanor Teece was very effective in a recitation" A bit of life."

DOUBLE BATHING FATALITY AT WARRINGTON.

Fatal Heroism.

A particularly distressing bathing fatality occurred at Warrington on Thursday afternoon, the victims being two youths named William Charles Head, aged eighteen, of 40, Lloyd-street, and Joseph Tavlin, aged sixteen, of G, Lloyd-street. At 4.30 the companions went to bathe in the St. Helens and Sankey Canal near Buttermilk Bridge. Head was the first to enter the water, and for a time he swam with the aid Of a cork belt. When in the middle of the canal he took off the belt, and tried to swim without it, but began to sink. He shouted for help, and Tavlin, who could swim a little, plunged into the canal with the intention of rescuing him. In his struggles, Head clutched Tavlin and dragged him under the water, and both youths were drowned. A boy raised the alarm, and the bodies were recovered by P.C. Dugdale. The fatality created a painful sensation in the Bank Quay neighbourhood, and many were the expressions of sympathy with the parents in their terribly sudden loss.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS

Cricket.

The splendid work which Lynes has done for the Warrington eleven, received some tangible recognition on Saturday, the match set apart for his benefit being a great success. A large crowd lined the Arpley enclos- ure, and was treated to a full afternoon's cricket. Manchester were the visitors and at twelve o'clock they entered upon their innings. After an interval for lunch, play was resumed, and it was not until seven o'clock that stumps were drawn. The result of the encounter was a draw, the position being fairly even. Manchester had compiled 236 for the loss of eight wickets and War- rington were 114 for three. The record of the locals is one to be proud of,and if theycan get through the season with only a solitary defeat they will be deserving of all praise. I hope their good form will be maintained until cricket has to make way for the exhilarating winter sport. So far the season of 1904 Ehas been gratifyingly successful.

The men from Cottonopolis proved good hitters, and despite the keen fielding and bowling, ran up sub- stantiartotals. J. Milne accomplished the best per- formance of the afternoon, knocking up 87 in vigorous style. His chief strokes included twelve fours. J. Heap, who had the honour of figuring in several county matches last season, was responsible for 25. He was smartly caught by Hampson. Sladen, the Manchester professional. compiled 23, Hampson stumping him from one of Podmore's deliveries. W. Phillips hit out merrily and made 27 not out, and L. Rowlands scored a creditable 18 and carried out his bat.

E. A. Connor was the most successful bowler,captur- ing three wickets for 37 runs. W. H. Podmore took two for 32, and Lynes two for 103.

Warrington had an hour and a half at their disposal, and they had no difficulty in saving the match. P. B. Hawtin was in brilliant form, and his contribution of 47 not out included one five and four fours. He is to be complimented upon his display. W. H. Podmore scored a useful 22, and J. W. Bibby placed 16 to his credit. Lynes made 18 before he was easily caught at point by Fielding.

To-day Bootle will be entertained at Arpley, and Warrington hope to repeat their feat of May 7th when they gained a brilliant victory at Bootle.

Lynes has been doing duty for Warwickshire against Worcestershire this week, and has performed very creditably with the ball. In the first innings he captured 3 wickets for 75 runs—the best bowling feat of the day —and in the second innings he was responsible for 3 wickets for 54 runs. He was not in very good form with the bat, and was bowled by Bird after making 3.

- Warrington Police scored a creditable victory at the expense of Liverpool Police Band on Thursday. P.C.'s Holland and Houghton bowled with great effect, taking 2 wickets for 9 runs, and 5 for 11 respectively.

Sankey Wire Mills and Hough Green were so evenly matched, that the encounter resulted in a tie of 61 runs each. The brothers Robert and N. Cook rendered good service for the Wire Mills, scoring 21 and 11 (not out) respectively. Warham (25) was the top batsman for the villagers.

Crosfleld's had hard luck in being defelikd by Moore and Daresbury by the margin of nine runs. Captain Wyatt knocked up 18 for the victors, T. Atherton (19) being in good form for the soaperies. S. Taylor captured seven wickets at the unusually low cost of 12 runs.

Crosfield's 2nd also came a cropper, their conquerors being Bold-street Wesleyans. Nelson (19) and Moor- field (14) achieved the best batting performances.

Stretton and Appleton triumphed ov6r Latchford Baptists by a 32 majority. F. Hatton (45) and A. Johnson (35 not out), rendered very valuable service for the victors, J. Cox and G. Cliffe with 20 and 26 respectively doing the best work for the Baptists.

Penketh cut up ignominiously against Grappenhall who trounced them to the extent of 74. J. Warburton ran up 36 for the winners, not a solitary Penketh player attainingiclouble figures. In fact, " Mr. Extras," with 7, was their highest scorer.

Moore and Daresbnry had little difficulty indisposing of Appleton and Stretton on Monday. So well did their men work that they won byan innings and 13 runs.C.B. Allen captured twelve wickets for 18 runs, and the Rev. F. Clifton Smith batted lane style for 38.

By a narrow margin Grappenhall accounted for Ashton Hayes, their best scorer being W. Carter who scored 38 in the two innings.

Warrington Thursday defeated the "Guardian "Office, the figures on each side being low. W. Bell (12) was the most successful batsman.

Bowling.

The works handicap opened on Saturday in ideal weather, and some good games were witnessed. The competition will be continued this afternoon on the King and Queen green.

Another victory by tie Liberal club was a notable feature of Saturday's league matches. Penketh were the visitors to Bewsey-street, and they returned van- quished by a 36 majority. There was a particularly close game between J. E. Warren and A. Woods, the last-named triumphing by a single point. J. Parr de- feated E. Bond by 15-13, and the same margin gave the victory to J. Bowyer over A. Greenhalgh.

In their encounter with Bull's Head the Liberals were unfortunate, suffering defeat by 15. One or two keenly fought games attracted Particular attention, J. Gibson defeating J. Worsley, jun., and. H. Williams accounting for Edward Shaw by single point majorities.

Bull's Head had an easier task before them on their own green, Brooklands being their opponents. The home men played with such skill as to pile on a majority of 111. The closest came was that in which John Worsley, jai:tr., beat W. Stevenson by 15-14.

The league table up to date is as follows : P. W. L. For Agst. Pts. Bull's Head Cemetery Horse and Jockey ll ••• 8, ••• 5 ...1653 ...1459 ... 12 Chapel House Brooklands Fearnhead Penketh Liberal Club l2 -. 4 -. 8 -.1703 -.1796 _.. 8

To-day's league matches are : Cemetery v. Brooklands. Cemetery v. Chapel House. Penketh Park.v. Horse and Jockey.

The annual president's main in connection with the Cemetery Club was played on Monday evening, and there was keen competition for the prizes presented by Mr. A. H.Crosfield. The winners were :-1, G.Ball ; 2, R. Young; 3, T. Healing; 4, A. E. Boardman; 5, T. Ellison, 6, J. Berry, 7, W. Povey ; 8, A. Podmore ; 9, T. Humphreys; 10, A. Stringer; 11, W. Gibson; 12, T. Jones; 13, A. Ball.

This afternoon the second annual, main in connection with the Prince of Wales Lodge of Oddfellows will take place on the Brooklands Green. The handsome silver cup and medal presented by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld will be competed for, in addition to sixteen prizes. There are 64 entries—exactly the same number as last year—and a pleasant afternoon is anticipated. Play commences at two o'clock. The green stewards will be Bros. H. Sykes, W. King, and W. Cottam, and the duties of referee will be discharged by Bro. T. Reddish.. The arrangements have been made by the following committee :—Bros. T. P. Bradshaw (chairman), T. Wadsworth, J. Massey, W. King, H. Sykes, E. Whitham, T. Reddish, H. Maddock and G. F. Freeman (secretary). The cup and prizes will be presented at a smoker to be held at the close of the season, and Mr. Crosfield is expected to be present.

The Liberal Club tnmdlers will hold their president's main this afternoon, and already there have been fifty entries. Mr. Crosfleld is giving some handsome prizes, and there are sure to be some splendid games.

===== 0002601_19040806_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
Cycling.

The Northwich Urban Council have treated lengths of main roads within their jurisdiction with westrumite to obviate the dust nuisance created by motor cars. They have also joined in the appeal to the County Council to have certain sections of road soheduled,with the object of preventing excessively fast driving. Can- not something be done in Warrington and district to mitigate the dust nuisance? Cyclists on some of the reads in the district have had strong reasons to com- plain of the lackadaisical methods of our local authori- ties in dealing with the nuisance.

The weather favoured the cyclists last week-end, for despite several heavy showers the conditions on the whole were excellent for toying. The Warrington Club's outing in Wales was highly successful, there being a representative turnout. The roads were in grand order. Llangollen was first visited, and after- wards the tourists went through Chirk and Oswestry to Ellesmere, where Sunday night was spent. Monday opened fine and bright, and the return was made via Overton, Erbistock Ferry, and Chester. The tour was thoroughly enjoyed by all.

About a dozen members had a spin tl Budworth via Arley on Tuesday evening, and on reaching the pic- turesque village a halt was made at the" George and Dragon," where a convivial hour was !pent. The return route was through,Stretton and Warrington was reached in good time.

She looked at him nervously, fearful as to how knew it at once. Is it too absurd on so shor',7 he would take the news. He didn't faint, he an-" didn't even grasp the mantelpiece for support. He She raised her head and looked at him wittv merely puffed a cloud of smoke and Smiled. She smiling eyes. was disappointed. Archie was so undemonstrative. "No, not too absurd," she said, softly. "Has she? By Jove!" he said. too knew it from the first ! " Ella was convinced he must be concealing his [THE END. I emotion. She began to admire him for it. She crept to his side and laid a sisterly touch on his "Poor old Arch !" she said, softly. "But that not the worst of it. It's all so utterly impossible." "Why ? Who's the chap ? " "Some creature she met by chance. Extremely handsome, I believe, but quite common, a working- man, a constructor, whatever that may be, in the l'enrosse Works, and named Smith into the bargaia. Carl Smith !" she finished, almost tragically. He was surprised this time. He let his cigarette drop through his fingers. "By Jove ! " he exclaimed. it all come about ? " • And she told him. "It's no good arguing with her," she went on "You know how romantic she is, and her craze for the Navy. Now, we can't let this thing go on. What are you going to do ? " • He had picked up his cigarette. button his remaining glove. "I must think it out as I go along," he said, moving towards the door. "So long. I shall be back by one." Ella looked after him. "Poor old boy ! " she murmured, pityingly. must have roughed him terribly, :Yet he didn't show it a bit." Outside in the street Lord Archie was striding along at a rapid pace. Suddenly he broke into a laugh. "Carl Smith, of the Penrosse Works, and little Nora! "he exclaimed to himself. "It's the strangest coincidence I've ever run across." A quarter of an hour afterwards he had arrived at the Grandley Hotel. He produced a card and handed it to the hall-porter. "Will you please send this up to the Countess Seydheim? " he said. The answer came down, and presently he wai conducted to a private suite of rooms on the firs; floor. As he entered a lady rose to meet him. • She was young, fair, and pretAy. She extended her hand to him with a smile of welcome. "You feel quite at home on land once more ?" he asked. "Quite. But aunt doesn't. She is confined tc her room with a sick headache and says we must manage to entertain one another." He looked up into her face and they both laughed. "Well, we had a good try once or twice on ; the ; boat, didn't we ? " Fie said, as he dropped into a .seat. She turned her head away and he noticed the prettiest shade imaginable spread over he' cheek. "I've got a most extraordinary thing to tell you," he said presently. "You remember my telling you about a friend of our family„ Lady "Oh, the the girl you're going to marry?" she broke in, with a mischievous smile. He edged a trifle nearer to her. "Not quite that. Listen. My little friend has met by chance, and fallen in love desperately, with a handsome rascal working in Penrosse's ship- building works." She turned to him •quickly - and laid a hand on. his aria. "Not-- ? " she cried. He nodded. "Yes, Carl Smith is his name, sure enough. And this is how it all came about." And once again the story of the carriage adventure was related. "And she's in love with him?" she exclaimed. "She inust be what you call 'romantic.'" "That's how my sister puts it. But all the same, Nora is one of the best girls I know, good throughout." "Is she?" she exclaimed, eagerly. "Yes, and I'll warrant you'll like her. Let me see, when can you meet ? Why, of course, at. Sir George Penrosse's to-morrow. You, Nora, Ella, and myself. By Jove! It will be quite a little comedy," he cried, excitedly. "With a happy ending ? " she queried, with a smile. He looked at her intently. He seemed to hesitate for a moment, then suddenly made a forward movement and took hold of her hand. • "Let's do our share towards it now," be whispered. "Anna, you must have guessed all the ! days we were together on the boat--" He paused, and looked into her eves. "Is it •--?" And she simply nodded.

GLASGOW AND EARLY CLOSING. Two Months' Experience. Glasgow has now had a couple of months' experience of universal ten o'clock closing of public-houses, and a comparison of the "drunks " and criminal cases with the corresponding period last year is very favourable to the new order of things. The diminution in the Mon- day" drunks "works out at an average of 37 per week, as the following statement shows : 1901. 1903 Decrease Monday, 30th May 127 ... 161 ... 34 „ 6th June ll5 .. 131 ... 36 „ 13th „ ~ 20th „ „ 27th f 7 4th July 164 ... 212 ... 48 If 11th „ „ 18th „ „ 25th „ • Ist Aug l3B ... 108 ... 1-30 1,197 1,535 338 Taking the returns for complete weeks the figures for " drunks " alone show an average decrease of 41 per week : 1904. 1903. WIGAN MURDER TRIAL. In the Crown Court at Liverpool Assizes on Tuesday, before Mr. Commissioner Lawrence, K.C., a Wigan collier named William Turner, 45, was indicted for the wilful murder of his wife on the second of May. It was stilted for the prosecution that on the day of the murder the prisoner had taken laudanum or opium, but an emetic was admimistered, and he was none the worse. Later in the even- ing cries were heard, and a neighbour try- ing to °pea the door, found it locked. Presently it was unlocked, and the prisoner came out, saying," I have done it ; I have finished her. You can go and fetch a policeman." The woman's death was due to hemorrhage from a wound in the throat. In evidence it was stated that the prisoner had been low-spirited, which was the reason he took the laudanum, and that some years ago he attempted to jump into a reservoir. Mr. Rigby Swift, his coursel, pleaded to the jury that there was a reasonable doubt as to the prisoner's sanity. The jury found that the prisoner was guilty, but was in- sane at the time he committed the crime, and he was ordered to be detained during the King's pleasure. • THE KEY TO HEALTH. Tuesday evening's destination will be Lymm. , The Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Club visited Southport on Bank Holiday, and spent a grand time at the popular resort. Leaving Sankey about seven o'clock, South- port was reached soon after ten. The various attrac- tions were patronized, and the homeward journey was safely accomplished by about ten o'clock. At the Runcorn Cycle Club Sports on Friday and Saturday, in the 120 yards handicap (open), A. Sinclair gained the second prize. J. Houghton, of Stockton Heath, vvasiflrst in the one mile and two mile bicycle handicaps, and is to be congratulated upon a fine performance. At the Runcorn Boating Club's Regatta, J. Peers, of Warrington S.C., was second in the 150 yards swimming' race (open). At the Horsham Athletic Club sports on Bank HotidaY a peat attraction was a two miles witch, in which A.. Shrubb, the one, four and ten miles amateur champion, met C. J. Straw, of Warrington, the northern junior cross-country champion. Shrubb conceded his opponent 100 yards start. Running in grand style, Shrubb rapidly decreased the gap. He drew level with his rival in the sixth lap, took the lead in the next circuitr and finally won easily in 9min. 33 1-ssecs. -- Football. • In another month we shall have entered on the foot- ball season. Clubs are already looking out for new' players. The Leigh club have been successful, so we aø' told in every Liverpool and Manchester paper, in making two important captures in South Wales. Ernest A. Cochlin, who has played half back for Cardiff during the last two seasons, and has also played to- Glamorgan- shire and in the international trial, has signed on for them. He is 22 years of age. Gamer Matthias, who has played full back for London Welsh and Maesteg and is 20 years old, has also signed. In addition W these two players, Leigh have also secured the services of Threlfall, the Morecambe centre three-quarter, and Houghton, a half back from a Runcorn junior club. Phil Thomas, the Oldham and Lancashire three-quarter back, has thrown in his lot with Leeds. It is state& that 'the transfer fee is the highest yet paid in N.T.J. football. The "Athletic News" football annual for 1904-5 h 0 made its appearance for the eighteenth successive year, and can be obtained on and after to-day (Saturday.) The guide consists of 228 pages and contains the results of last season, and all the official. fixtures of the various leagues for the campaign about to commence, lists of international players, the laws of the Association and Northern Union games, and much other valuable information. The Portraits are those of Mr. T. BaddeleY (Wolverhampton Wanderers and England), H. Burgess (Manchester City and England), P. S. Hancock (Rich- mond and England), James Watson Sunderland and Scotland), L. AT. McLeod (Cambridge University and Scotland), R. Atherton (Middlesbrough), and Joseph Wallace (Ireland). The Secretaries' Directory will he" found more extensive than ever. WARRINGTON AUDITORS' REPORT- The Outstanding Accounts. The Warrington borough aulitors presented the fol- lowing report at the last, meeting of the finance Cora- mittee Warrington. 26th July, 1901. The Mayor, Aldermen and Burgesses of the County Borough of Warrington. Gentlemen,— BOROUGH AUDIT. We have audited the books of account of the Corpora- tion for the year ended 31st March, 1904, and subject to our remarks below certify them to be correct. The securities for Investments have been produced to us and we have found the same to be in order. By an Order of the Council, dated 6th February,- 1990, the heads of the departments were instructed to forward all accounts for materials supplied and work done for the Corporation during the previous financial year to the Borough Treasurer, as soon es-, possible after the 31st March. A copy of this reso- lution was sent to each department by the Borough Treasurer early in March this year, with a request ' that it should be acted upon, but insufficient notice appears to have been taken of it, as we find that out- standings amounting to a considerable sum were not includ,,d in the accounts under review. The Corporation officials have rendered everi assistance, arid we have pleasure in stating that the' books are kept in a very satisfactory manner. We are, gentlemen, yours faithfullj , JAMES FAIROLOUGH, Mayor's Auditor. EDWIN BRADSHAW,Chartered Accountant,i Elective . JAMES BALL, Auditor The Borough Treasurer reported that the " outstand' rugs" referred to by the auditors amounted to £2,700. £1,113 of which represents accounts due to the Gas' Electricity and Water Departments ; and after explana- tion from the Borough Treasurer and Borough SurveYor as to the difficulty of getting in accounts, it Wes resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to direct the attention of the head officials of each department to the' resolution of the Council of the 6th February, 1900, and to request them to insist upon the intentions of the' resolution being carried out in future. YESTERDAY'S PARLIAMENT. Scene In the House. A scene occurred in House of Commons last evening on Mr. Balfour moving the closure of the Education (Local Authority Default) Bill. The whole of the OP position, led by Mr. Asquith, left the House in a body'. Among the members named by Mr. Lowther as disre; garding the authority of the Chair, were Mr. Ilarwoon and Mr. Lloyd George. The Bill then passed through Committee. Death.
